West Brom told to ‘find the money’ and sign Blackburn Rovers star

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

EFL pundit Tom Garratt has urged West Brom to ‘find the money’ to sign Blackburn Rovers ace Andri Gudjohnsen during the January transfer window.

Speaking on the latest edition of the Pyramid Podcast, Garratt, hosting alongside Callum Fowler, believes Gudjohnsen could be the missing piece in the Albion jigsaw to help the Black Country outfit seal promotion back to the Premier League.

James Morrison’s men continue to be in the promotion conversation, heading into the latest international break unbeaten in their last six games and securing four victories in that period.

Their latest 3-0 romping of Millwall last weekend leaves Albion fifth in the second-tier standings, sitting just one point off the summit in such a compact top eight.

Much was made at the beginning of the season about West Brom’s lack of a clinical frontman, with it one of the main concerns as to why some connected with Albion feel a promotion push may fade away in time.

Controlling shareholder Shilen Patel may be able to fix the issue in the January transfer window, should funds become available, and it could well be the difference maker in whether they return to the big time or not.

So, with this in mind, well-known EFL pundit and West Brom supporter Tom Garratt has demanded the club make a deal to sign one of the best-performing strikers in the Championship, at this moment in time.

Despite having such an excellent summer transfer window, one criticism from some West Brom supporters at the club was not going all out to recruit a striker who could score regularly at Championship level.

Albion were reportedly interested in Southampton’s Cameron Archer, but missed out on a deal due to his high wages on the South Coast, with the 24-year-old eventually going on to move to French outfit Auxerre instead, where he has scored six goals in eight Ligue 1 games.

The Baggies were busy during the summer window revamping some of the forward line, replacing the outgoings of Josh Maja and Daryl Dike with Barney Stewart and Jimmy-Jay Morgan.

Morgan in particular has been a roaring success so far, grabbing eight goal contributions in 15 league matches so far for the club, but is not a conventional centre-forward, favouring drifting over to the left-hand side while a proper number nine stays central.

Stewart, since moving from Scottish side Falkirk, has found opportunities hard to come by and has netted just once for his new club so far, while Aune Heggebo in his second season at the club has also found himself in and out of the side, with most of his success coming off the bench and making an impact in recent times.

Thankfully, much of Albion’s early success has been down to players in other positions chipping in with goals, with the likes of Isaac Price notching six times and providing three assists, while fellow summer arrival Felix Horn Myhre now has three goals to his name in the blue and white stripes.

West Bromwich Albion signed Felix Horn Myhre for just £3.1 million in the summer ahead of Spanish outfit Elche

Despite that, Albion fans would be put at ease to see a clinical frontman lead the line from January onwards, with a regular source of goals badly needed for the Baggies to continue to fight towards the top end of the division.

Speaking on the latest episode of the Pyramid Podcast with Callum Fowler, EFL pundit Tom Garratt has demanded West Brom ‘find the money’ to sign Blackburn Rovers star Andri Gudjohnsen in January.

Gudjohnsen is already into double figures for goals from his opening 14 league games this season, notably scoring a brace at The Hawthorns against West Brom last month, while also netting in Rovers' 2-1 victory over West Ham last weekend.

And Garratt was gushing about the 24-year-old's strike against the Hammers, believing he is the second-best striker in the division and can help the Baggies turn their promotion hopes into a reality.

Five players, five careers. Name them from the clubs they played for.

Each day there are five current or former EFL players to identify. For each one you’ll see their senior clubs in the order they played for them. Loan spells are left out.

You get four guesses per player. Every wrong guess unlocks a clue: first their position, then their nationality, then their initials.

Get it first time for 4 points, then 3, 2 and 1. The best possible score is 20. A new set of players arrives at midnight.

Garratt said: “This is a plea to West Bromwich Albion again.

“Find the money, find the money somewhere, it’s there somewhere. (Tom) Fellows went for a lot of money. We have sold players in recent years, go and buy (Andri) Gudjohnsen.

“His movement for the goal is so, so good. Don’t get me wrong, it’s a ‘tap in’, but his movement to get there is unbelievable. He is the second-best striker in this league.”

There’s no doubt that what’s missing most in West Brom’s squad is the addition of a striker that can usually net between 20 and 25 goals in a full Championship season, with Albion continuing to bank on the likes of Isaac Price to perform and find the back of the net consistently.

But over time, that’s at risk of drying up, so Albion must do all they can at the turn of the new year to bring in a forward who has an eye for goal and can make a real difference at the top end of the pitch.

And while it’s likely to be one of the areas the Baggies hierarchy look to address, the chances of Blackburn’s Andri Gudjohnsen signing on the dotted line at The Hawthorns is bound to be extremely low.

West Brom fans, could you see the Baggies moving for Andri Gudjohnsen in January, or do you feel he's an unrealistic target? Drop your thoughts in the comments below!

After having much of a revamp during the summer transfer window, Albion are likely to not possess many financial funds in January, with clubs across the country much more active in the loan market as bigger sides to themselves decide to temporarily part ways with players who can’t break into their first-team crop.

Meanwhile, Gudjohnsen’s impact at Blackburn cannot be understated. His 10 goals so far have been a massive reason as to why Rovers sit on the periphery of the top eight, and while the Lancashire outfit have let go of some stars on the cheap in recent times, there’s no reason to suggest why Blackburn wouldn’t want a massive fee to part them from their Icelandic star - a price that only Premier League level sides can afford.

You do feel if Gudjohnsen was to move on from Ewood Park in January, then it would be permanently, so factoring in what would be such a high price to secure his services and an unwillingness to let him leave on loan, in particular to a Championship rival, you would think it’s a very low chance that West Brom will be Gudjohnsen’s next club.

However, there’s no reason why Albion shouldn’t be able to land a similar goalscoring forward on loan in January, with the winter window always throwing up such unexpected deals the closer the clock ticks towards the deadline.

Zoë Kravitz Is Back on the Red Carpet, Going for Gold, And Baring Her Abs

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

What does one wear to the premiere of a movie called How to Rob a Bank? If you’re Zoë Kravitz, it’s got to be something worth stealing. The actor made a glittering return to the red carpet in New York last night, attending the premiere of her new film alongside co-stars Nicholas Hoult and Pete Davidson. The actor opted for suitably decadent, gilded gold Saint Laurent ensemble, with plenty of skin on show—in one fell swoop, hitting two of her red carpet rules.

Kravitz wore a cropped, long-sleeved jacket in richly patterned gold brocade, with an embellished neckline and plush, dark brown faux-fur cuffs. Sitting low on her hips were matching straight-leg trousers, with intricate crystal embellishments and a sparkling floral buckle at the waistband. Barely-there metallic sandals, delicate earrings, and an undone updo with wispy tendrils framing her face finished things off. And there was a generous flash of those famously toned abs.

Kravitz has been all over New York of late, filming her forthcoming movie Die Alive alongside Jamie Dornan and Riley Keough while also enjoying some downtime with fiancé Harry Styles. Her off-duty uniform remains resolutely relaxed: The Row trousers, low-slung denim, little T-shirts, and well-loved outerwear. But when it comes to the red carpet, Kravitz has a rather different set of sartorial rules: Saint Laurent, lots of skin, and layers of gold.

At the CFDA Awards earlier this month, Kravitz joined fellow house muses Hailey Bieber, Rosé, and Anja Rubik in celebrating Anthony Vaccarello, who received the International Designer of the Year award. She wore a shimmering, liquid-gold, open-backed look from the spring 2027 collection, with a high neckline and a slinky, low-waisted skirt. Of course, Kravitz is no stranger to a little midriff exposure, either: Who could forget the ultra-low-slung, ab-baring recreation of a spring 1990 Yves Saint Laurent look, originally worn by Yasmeen Ghauri, that she wore for the Caught Stealing premiere last year?

Whether its molten metallics or the tiniest sliver of fabric, Zoë Kravitz knows exactly what works for her on the red carpet.

Michael Fassbender’s 2-Part Spy Thriller Officially in Jeopardy With No Renewal in 2026

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Spy thrillers have enjoyed a massive boom in recent years, with almost every major streaming platform committing significant investment to fuel the allure. Paramount+ has perhaps made the biggest bang for its buck thanks to two major hits in Taylor Sheridan's Lioness and The Agency, starring Michael Fassbender. The latter originally premiered in 2024 as an instant smash hit, earning a swift Season 2 renewal. That second chapter arrived in the summer, with improved quality, and after a cliffhanger ending, fans have been left wondering about its future.

The Agency Season 2 premiered with all 10 episodes released at once on Paramount+. The new season's critical and audience reception scores both saw significant bumps, from 66% to 92% and 79% to 93%, respectively. Though arriving amid stiff streaming competition, The Agency still managed to pull in 393 million minutes watched in its debut week, per Nielsen data, which ranked it the 9th most-watched show at the time. Paramount+, however, has yet to publish the season's official streaming numbers, which will offer better insight into its performance against other Paramount originals — a key metric that will affect its future.

Paramount+'s silence on The Agency Season 3 is rather concerning, especially considering that Season 2 was renewed mere weeks after the Season 1 premiere. The delay could, however, be due to Paramount and Warner Bros.' recent merger under Skydance. Given that such mergers often involve significant cost-cutting measures, there's no telling where this leaves The Agency. Also, offering more cause for concern is a recent update from series star Dominic West, who plays the CIA director. During an interview with The Playlist, he revealed that The Agency was facing a delay, saying, "I was talking to someone about it last night, and I think there was some delay about it for some reason."

Britain makes more detective television than any country has any business making, and somehow almost all of it is good. Here is the one you belong in, what it says about you, and the case for it being the best of the four.

You want the tea, the gossip and the vicar behaving oddly. Midsomer is a county where the scenery is beautiful, the fete is on Saturday and the murder rate is roughly that of a small war, and absolutely nobody involved thinks this is strange.

You feel other people's grief as if it were handed to you personally. Broadchurch is less a whodunnit than a study of what a small town does to itself when it finds out that the answer was always going to be somebody everybody already knew.

You go through things rather than around them. John Luther is the best detective in London and a liability to everybody who works with him, and the show is completely honest that those two facts are the same fact.

You say the thing and you do not dress it up. Vera Stanhope turns up in a hat and a mac, is rude to almost everybody, and is the only person in the room prepared to ask the question that the family has been waiting years for somebody to ask.

West, in the same interview, which primarily centered on his new HBO Max series, War, could not offer details as to the reason for the delay. He, however, expressed hopes that it was something the series could somehow navigate. "I don't know... But hopefully there will, yeah. I think we need to see more of the new head of the CIA," he said.

Should The Agency eventually earn a renewal, the likelihood of its return in 2027 now appears nearly impossible, given that Season 2 arrived about a year and a half after it was renewed. Even though Paramount+ announces the renewal before the year ends, 2028 would be a more plausible release year going by the established release timeline.

The Agency's first two seasons are streaming on Paramount+. Stay tuned for future updates.

Home Depot vs. Uber: One Stock Is Betting on a Housing Recovery, the Other on the Future of Transportation. Which Is the Better Buy for 2027?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Strong earnings growth, attractive valuations, and an uncertain economic outlook are creating two very different opportunities for investors heading into 2027.

Home Depot (HD -0.69%) and Uber Technologies (UBER -1.57%) are two market leaders facing very different challenges heading into 2027. Home Depot is navigating a prolonged housing slowdown, while Uber continues to deliver strong growth despite a struggling stock price and growing uncertainty about the future of autonomous transportation.

For investors, the distinction between temporary economic headwinds and potential long-term disruption could be critical in determining which stock offers the better opportunity in the upcoming year.

Home Depot is a home improvement retailer that serves homeowners, contractors, and construction professionals. The company has increasingly focused on expanding its professional contractor business through acquisitions to strengthen its presence in roofing, building materials, and specialty construction products. This strategy helps diversify revenue beyond traditional do-it-yourself home improvement spending.

In fiscal 2025, Home Depot generated $164.7 billion in revenue, an increase of 3.2% year over year. However, net income declined 4.4% to $14.2 billion as elevated borrowing costs and sluggish housing activity weighed on demand. More recent results highlight continued challenges. In the second quarter of fiscal 2026, revenue increased 5.7% to $47.9 billion, but comparable sales grew just 1.7%, indicating relatively modest underlying demand despite contributions from acquisitions.

Home Depot remains a substantial cash-generating business, producing approximately $12.6 billion in free cash flow during fiscal 2025. However, its debt-to-equity ratio of roughly 5.1x reflects a leveraged balance sheet, while its current ratio stood near 1.1x. Although its cash generation provides financial stability, meaningful growth will likely depend on an improvement in housing activity and renewed spending on larger renovation projects.

Uber operates a global mobility and delivery platform connecting millions of consumers with drivers, couriers, and merchants. The company is positioning itself for the transition to autonomous transportation, focusing on integrating self-driving vehicles into its existing network rather than developing its own autonomous driving technology. Management expects more than 2,000 fully driverless vehicles operating on its platform by the end of 2027.

In fiscal 2025, Uber generated $52 billion in revenue, an 18% year-over-year increase. Net income reached approximately $10.1 billion, although a substantial portion reflected a one-time tax benefit. More recent results highlight continued operating momentum. In the third quarter of 2026, gross bookings increased 20% to $59.8 billion, while revenue climbed 10% to $14.8 billion. Adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) rose 31% to nearly $3 billion, demonstrating improving profitability as the platform expands.

Uber also maintains a strong financial position. In fiscal 2025, the company generated $9.8 billion in free cash flow, an increase of 42% year over year. At the end of September 2026, it held approximately $10.1 billion in unrestricted cash and short-term investments, providing substantial flexibility to invest in new technologies and expand its operations.

Home Depot's biggest challenge remains the housing market. Elevated mortgage rates and sluggish home sales have weighed on renovation spending, while competition from other home improvement retailers adds pressure on pricing and margins. Although lower interest rates could eventually revive demand, a prolonged housing slowdown could continue limiting growth.

Uber faces a potentially more significant long-term challenge from autonomous transportation. While the company is positioning its platform to accommodate self-driving vehicles, competitors could increasingly offer rides directly to consumers, threatening Uber's market share and pricing power. Regulatory uncertainty surrounding driver classification, along with competition from Lyft (LYFT +1.55%) and other mobility providers, introduces additional risks.

Uber appears to offer better value based on future earnings estimates, though Home Depot remains the more affordable option when comparing its price to trailing sales.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

Home Depot and Uber offer two very different investment opportunities heading into 2027. The distinction comes down to the nature of their challenges. Home Depot's struggles are largely cyclical, meaning a recovery in housing activity could revive demand for home improvement projects. However, with mortgage rates remaining elevated and remodeling spending expected to grow modestly, a meaningful rebound may take time.

Uber, meanwhile, is already delivering strong operating results. Its latest quarterly gross bookings increased 20%, while adjusted EBITDA climbed 31%. Despite that momentum, the stock has struggled as investors question how autonomous vehicles could reshape its business. That uncertainty is legitimate, particularly as self-driving operators explore offering rides directly to consumers. However, Uber's established customer network, growing delivery business, and substantial cash generation provide competitive advantages as the industry evolves.

For investors looking toward 2027, Uber is my preferred buy. Trading at 20.4 times forward earnings compared with Home Depot's 23.3 times, Uber offers a compelling combination of faster growth and a slightly lower earnings valuation. Autonomous transportation poses meaningful long-term risks, but I believe the company's current operating momentum and financial strength make a stronger investment case than waiting for an uncertain housing recovery.

‘Wake-Up Call’: Trump’s Former Housing Commissioner Says New Congress Must ‘Get Serious’ About Housing Affordability

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Democrats and Republicans both understand Americans are broadly worried about the cost of living, and a former Trump administration official says it's time for both parties to take action.

In a bitterly divided Washington, DC, it remains to be seen whether lawmakers will heed that call when the 120th Congress convenes in January, with Democrats back in control of both the House and Senate.

After campaigning hard on affordability concerns, Democratic leaders say they'll use their new majorities to focus on the cost of living. Republican leaders admit they didn't heed voter concerns about the economy. Meanwhile, President Donald Trump has threatened to use his veto power freely.

The summer's 21st Century Road to Housing Act, with its dozens of provisions aimed at cutting red tape, was a rare example of bipartisan consensus. What follows that landmark legislation remains an open question.

Republicans want more deregulation while Democrats aim for more support for housing. And each side must now contend with an upswell of hard-liners pushing policies that could radically change the market.

Many of the prominent names in both parties who built the housing bill are returning, including Rep. French Hill (R-AR), Rep. Maxine Waters (D-CA), Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-MA), and Sen. Tim Scott (R-SC). They're likely to return to the House and Senate committees on housing and banking.

"Now is a wake-up call," Trump's former Federal Housing Commissioner Frank Cassidy tells Realtor.com®. "Senate, House, White House. Everyone needs to get serious about tackling and addressing housing affordability. It can't be ignored anymore."

Congress had some bipartisan momentum to provide tax incentives that encourage the buying and selling of homes.

Colin Allen, executive director of the American Property Owners Alliance and a former housing issues adviser to Sen. Lindsey Graham (R-SC), said that could mean some ideas pitched in this Congress could come back as part of larger bills.

"They have good working relationships and have already done some bipartisan work," Allen said.

Take, for instance, the Neighborhood Homes Investment Act, which would create a new tax credit to buy and renovate homes in economically distressed areas. The bill drew 70 sponsors from both parties.

Real estate groups put in significant groundwork this year to update the capital gains tax scheme for the first time in more than 30 years. The More Homes on the Market Act came close to 200 sponsors in Congress. It would double the exclusion for many filers. It's been floating around Congress for several years and has been a target of the National Association of Realtors® and other groups.

Trump expressed some support for changing the capital gains tax scheme, though a specific plan hasn't yet materialized from the White House.

"We've done a lot of legwork to get folks behind it," Allen said. "If we're looking at a larger bipartisan bill, you can see a scenario where a few of those ideas get together. The debt limit is coming up, and there are a few other opportunities to package together."

Hill and Republicans have lobbied for more changes to the financing system. They were able to get several banking reform provisions into the Road to Housing bill. They've also floated new financial reforms to change and rescind banking rules codified in the Dodd-Frank Act and the Basel III accords after the subprime mortgage crisis. Republicans say they've chased smaller banks out of the housing market.

This summer, Hill called Dodd-Frank a "massive overreaction."

"For our smaller banks, the new regulations were a poor fit, burying them in compliance work and bureaucratic red tape," Hill said. "Instead of helping customers, their attention was diverted to filling out forms and satisfying examiners."

Mortgage reform is also a lever Congress can pull. A bipartisan group of six members of Congress pitched the First-Time Homebuyer Affordability Act this summer. It would expand access to low-interest mortgages for first-time homebuyers.

"By expanding access to financing, we can help more hardworking families achieve homeownership, strengthen our communities, and build long-term financial security," said Rep. Darin LaHood (R-IL) said.

Then there's the matter of new construction. Democrats and Republicans found some bipartisan ground in cutting some of the federal red tape that blocks construction of new homes.

Republicans also said they favor rolling back Build America Buy America provisions. Builders, they say, have complained that the sourcing requirements are time-consuming and expensive. But they've also targeted some environmental regulations that Democrats passed.

Dennis Shea, chair of the J. Ronald Terwilliger Center for Housing Policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, tells Realtor.com that some public housing authorities saw costs climb by double digits. The U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development's waiver process is every bit as time-consuming for builders.

"A sentiment exists in both parties to change the rules, but ultimately it takes an act of Congress to exempt housing," Shea said.

Hill and other lawmakers such as Rep. Emanuel Cleaver (D-MO) have said that Congress' action should shift some burden onto states to implement reforms at the local and state level. The American Enterprise Institute said this year that state and local policy changes could boost annual homebuilding by six figures. But many of those new homes are accessory dwelling units.

Congress and the White Hose also found common ground on manufactured housing reform. All sides recognize they need to keep eyeing supply-side reforms, says Cassidy.

"Ultimately, the problem in this country is supply," Cassidy adds. "You could lower rates to 2% tomorrow, and all that will do is increase home values. It's not going to solve the underlying issue."

The other big question will be funding of the many provisions created under the Road to Housing Act. While the provisions in the bill were bipartisan, Congress must still approve funding for some of those programs. Some of those discussions are coming in the 2028 fiscal budget and beyond.

Over the months of negotiations on the Road to Housing bill, some Republicans took issue with unfunded mandates in it. Concerns about funding programs, and at what level, could spill over into congressional proceedings in coming years.

Tony Marquez, director of American University's real estate program, who has a long career in real estate and banking, tells Realtor.com that the industry is still amenable to a few different ideas for how to solve housing. It doesn't rely on one side's control of the government.

"My un-professional assessment is we'll have a certain amount of gridlock over a number of years where Democrats may come up with some suggestions," Marquez says. "But the answer to affordable housing is not just one silver bullet. It can have many elements."

Tristan Navera is a senior reporter on housing policy, covering trends and solutions in the housing market from Washington, DC. He was previously a senior reporter at Bloomberg Law, and before that covered real estate for the Washington Business Journal. Earlier in his career, he spent a decade reporting on business and real estate in Dayton and Columbus, OH. A Cincinnati native, he holds a journalism degree from Ohio University.

Torino, riqualificazione stadio: i prossimi step. La proprietà Cairo può cambiare

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Svolta storica per il futuro del Torino e del suo impianto di casa: il club granata ha ufficialmente depositato in Comune la proposta di fattibilità per la riqualificazione, valorizzazione e gestione dello stadio Olimpico Grande Torino e del centro sportivo Robaldo. Il piano, elaborato in sinergia con il Politecnico di Torino secondo la normativa del partenariato pubblico-privato, contempla diverse opzioni progettuali per l'ammodernamento delle strutture e lo sviluppo di nuove aree commerciali. Il deposito mette fine a mesi di pressing e solleciti da parte della Giunta guidata dal sindaco Stefano Lo Russo, giunti fino all'ultimatum fissato per il 31 dicembre, aprendo di fatto l'iter burocratico volto all'assegnazione del diritto di superficie che potrebbe estendersi fino a 99 anni.

Il percorso amministrativo entra ora nella fase di analisi tecnica da parte degli uffici comunali, attesi tra la fine dell'anno e gennaio ad esprimersi sulla fattibilità del progetto per arrivare alla formale dichiarazione di pubblica utilità. Una volta approvata la delibera di Giunta, la Città potrà indire tra la fine dell'inverno e la primavera il bando di evidenza pubblica - della durata stimata di circa 60 giorni - basato sulla proposta presentata dal club, con l'obiettivo di giungere all'aggiudicazione definitiva entro la fine dell'estate o l'inizio dell'autunno. Lo riferisce TuttoSport.

Oltre a rappresentare un passaggio cruciale per le infrastrutture sportive cittadine, l'Operazione Stadio assume un peso determinante per gli assetti societari del Torino. La prospettiva di un impianto moderno e da gestire a lungo termine è destinata ad attrarre investitori, fondi immobiliari e multinazionali esterne, interessati a rilevare la società o ad affiancare la proprietà nella ristrutturazione. Uno scenario che si sposa perfettamente con le intenzioni del presidente Urbano Cairo, pronto a cedere il club come confermato dal mandato affidato al colosso bancario statunitense Bank of America per la ricerca di nuovi acquirenti.

A tale of two families: Why one left Broward schools — and another stayed

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The reasons for coming or going are as varied as families themselves, but learning what drives their decisions can help public schools retain students.

This is the second installment of a three-part series on the impact of private school choice on public school enrollment.

To better understand parents' school choice decisions, we interviewed two Broward County, Florida, families — the Frys and the Gibson-Petersons. While each family has their own reasons for leaving or staying in the district's public schools, learning about these families' experiences may be helpful to the broader school choice discussion.

When Nancy Fry was a student in Florida's Broward County Public Schools in the mid 1990s to early 2000s, a population surge meant that the district was literally bursting out of its buildings. Fry's high school, in fact, had so many students that her entire 9th grade learned in portable classrooms.

Now, as she raises her two children in the same district, she's watching how underenrolled some schools are becoming. As a volunteer on school advisory boards, Fry has a front row seat to the struggles Broward faces as reduced funding is leading to staff layoffs and school closures.

"I saw — from one year to the next — my daughter's elementary school lose almost $4 million in funding just because of enrollment," said Fry, who is also a community organizer for Families for Strong Public Schools, a nonprofit that advocates for high-quality public schools across Florida.

Even with the reduced staffing and funding, Fry said, the public school system provides more learning and extracurricular opportunities for her children than private schools would. Her son is in 9th grade and participates in the international Cambridge program, an academically rigorous curriculum.

Additionally, her son has access to college-level courses, Advanced Placement classes, sports, theater and other activities. Fry's daughter is a 5th grader in the gifted program. A benefit of the public school system is that it must educate all students, regardless of ability, income or other factors, Fry said.

"Public schools are the great equalizer," she said. "Everyone has access to the same type of education, regardless of your income and your ZIP code."

At the same time, Fry said she knows public school is not a perfect fit for everybody.

Still, she said she knows Broward County Public Schools provides a quality education, and added that she's hopeful the district can attract more students and be more financially secure.

"Just having the stability of not worrying about: Is there going to be funding? Is there going to be a state takeover? Is the school going to have to close? Not having to worry about that, I think, would also help draw people back into the public schools," Fry said.

She also said Florida should invest more in its public school systems.

"I hope that the state decides to prioritize our students again and fund our public schools throughout the state accordingly, not just for Broward, but every school district. I believe every school district should get funding to meet the needs of the students, and that we can do better by our kids and make sure that they don't have to struggle to get what they need."

After two years of her daughter being bullied in 2nd and 3rd grade at her Broward County public school, Simone Gibson-Peterson enrolled her daughter in a private Christian school with tuition assistance from Florida's Hope Scholarship program for students who have experienced bullying, harassment, assault and other violations.

The Hope scholarship is now part of the state's universal Family Empowerment Scholarship program.

"I should not have to send my child to school and worry about her coming home feeling intimidated or not wanting to go back to school, and that was my biggest problem. Like, I cried every day having to watch my daughter go back into the same situation that she left the day before," said Gibson-Peterson. The mother said that as a student in Broward County Public Schools in the late 1980s, she herself was bullied and got into fights.

In the 12th grade, Gibson-Peterson dropped out of school, but later earned a GED and continued her education. She eventually earned a master's degree in criminal justice with a concentration in child abuse.

Her daughter, now a 7th grader, is thriving at her new school, Gibson-Peterson said. "There's so much accountability. They have zero tolerance for bullying, like that's not even going to happen," she said.

Her daughter's grades improved, and she is flourishing socially, her mom said. The school provides tutoring and extracurricular activities, as well as extra time for tests for her daughter, who has ADHD. "She's into track, she's into cheerleading, and she's just overall fully involved in school and happy about it."

The $8,000-a-year scholarship doesn't fully meet the $16,000 annual tuition, so Gibson-Peterson and her ex-husband split the expense, which comes to about $560 each a month. But, she said, the tuition covers most costs, including field trips.

Even though Gibson-Peterson is pleased with her family's decision to leave the public school system and enroll in private school, she said that because she works in child protection services, she's a firm supporter of a healthy and safe public school system.

The children she works with "need the public school system, but I also believe in school choice because not all of the schools have great teachers or great staff," Gibson-Peterson said.

"I want to be able to know that when I send my children — not just my biological child but my children that live in my foster care village — I want to know that when I send them to school, that they're safe."

3 ways public schools are competing for enrollment

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

School leaders are borrowing pages from marketing and advertising executives to develop tactics that will rightsize their enrollment.

This is the third installment of a three-part series on the impact of private school choice on public school enrollment.

The decision to close 129-year-old Perry Elementary School in Belvidere, Ill., earlier this year devastated many community members who didn't want to see their historic building shuttered.

While scores of schools across the country are closing as enrollment declines empty their classrooms, that was not the case with Perry. The 174-student school, in the end, just got old. Its aging structure needed a prohibitive $14 million to $16 million in repairs, according to Cassandra Schug, superintendent of Belvidere Community Unit School District 100.

The district's enrollment has in fact remained relatively stable, with only a modest decline in recent years, Schug says. Enrollment stands at 7,348 students in 2026, down from 7,470 in 2021.

Still, the decision to close Perry and the reaction it engendered from the community offer constructive guidance for administrators everywhere who are staring down school closures brought on by shrinking student populations.

When Perry's closure became inevitable after a 6-1 school board vote in April, the community rallied to usher its beloved school out in style, Schug says. Longtime teacher Susan Thomas spearheaded a committee to honor the school’s legacy with a citywide celebration ultimately attended by hundreds of people in May.

Thomas, who taught 5th grade at Perry for 19 years, says the neighborhood school was “so much more” than just a building. The closure decision, she says, was nothing short of traumatic.

Perry “has been a cornerstone for Belvidere. It needed to be celebrated. It needed us to be able to show our appreciation for what it had meant for so many years, and I just wanted it to be done right,” Thomas says.

The celebration allowed people across the community to visit and view old yearbooks, photos, letters, newspaper articles and student projects — all organized by decade and displayed on tables and walls throughout the gym.

“So you could walk through and just see all of that Perry history and recognize how education had changed over the years, how the building had changed over the years, and again, just really spend time sort of saying goodbye,” Schug says.

The celebration “helped us all to process what had been a really difficult time," honor the school's legacy, "create that closure, and give us a chance to celebrate those positive memories that Perry had brought to the community.”

Beginning this school year, Perry's staff members and students dispersed to other schools in the district, with students transferring through a lottery system.

Though Belvidere enrollment is expected to stay steady, district officials are carefully watching enrollment numbers and projections, especially as more public schools nationwide experience declines, Schug says.

And, she adds, she hopes she never has to close another school in her career again.

As some school communities grieve shuttered schools, districts are proactively working to boost enrollments and stave off closures. Here are three examples of how school leaders are striving to stay competitive.

The days of traditional public school systems having a monopoly on education and just worrying about teaching students are gone, says Brian Stephens, CEO of Caissa K12, a Memphis-based strategic communications firm.

In 2009, Stephens co-founded a public strategy firm that specialized in complex communication crisis management. But in 2017, as he saw more states adopt private school choice programs, the firm converted to focusing exclusively on student recruitment and retention for traditional public schools.

Currently, Caissa K12 — named for the mythical goddess of chess and strategy — works with nearly 100 districts in 26 states. The company says it has helped recruit about 55,000 students for its clients through research, communications, events and knocking on doors.

The firm is one of a growing number of players in this space.

Taking a page from the retail industry, one of Caissa K12's services for its district clients is performing "secret shopper" outreach. Caissa K12 employees or contractors act as families interested in enrolling their child and visit or connect with public and private schools to look for enrollment or communication barriers or other customer service problems.

The company works with school districts including Florida's Broward County Public Schools through performance contracts, meaning Caissa K12 only gets paid if the company meets an agreed-upon student recruitment goal.

When it comes to recruiting and retaining students, Stephens says, the greatest mistakes public schools make are not advocating for themselves and not being willing to improve flaws like hard-to-navigate websites.

Those steps may sound easy, but they are difficult for school systems with a lot on their plates, he says.

If the government and the people want public schools to "act like a business instead of a public good, then we have to act more like a business," Stephens says. "That means we need to advocate for ourselves."

Districts that are innovative and want to build trust with families "absolutely have to reach out and let people know that they exist and let people know how awesome they are," he says.

Stephens points to creative approaches public schools can take to help attract and keep students.

One district client, he notes, installed a washer and dryer at a school to support school attendance by making sure children could have clean clothes. Other schools have provided feminine hygiene products, which also boosted school attendance, Stephens says.

Eliminating outdated and abrasive policies can make a difference too. For instance, schools need to remove attendance policies that punish students for having overdue library books or unpaid fees for band uniforms, he says.

“Stepping up those things has really helped. You have to identify what the issue is that's stopping a child from attending.”

A new federal school choice program launching next year has many public school communities wondering how their students might benefit from billions of dollars in expected donations for K-12 educational expenses.

Nevada's Clark County School District, for example, is considering a collaboration with its Public Education Foundation, which would serve as a scholarship-granting organization to direct donations to benefit the Las Vegas-area district's students. Those extra scholarship funds could be targeted toward career-connected learning opportunities, technology, tutoring and more, according to an Aug. 13 presentation to the district's board of trustees.

Elsewhere, public school advocates from Iowa to Denver are also making plans to direct some of these taxpayer contributions to public schools.

And at least one new national scholarship-granting organization has formed to funnel donations to services supporting public school students. The Future School Fund, founded by Deborah Gist, former Rhode Island commissioner of education and state education superintendent in the District of Columbia, intends to help schools identify programs where scholarship funds can be used.

The federal Education Freedom Tax Credit program, which launches on Jan. 1, will guide taxpayer donations to students attending either public or private schools, who can then use the scholarships to pay for allowable educational expenses.

This first-ever nationwide school choice program is projected to raise nearly $26 billion annually for about 2.2 million scholarships by 2030, according to the administering agencies, the U.S. Department of the Treasury and the IRS.

This could overshadow the nation's current largest education program, Title I, which has an $18.4 billion budget for fiscal year 2026 budget to aid children at low-income schools. Title I serves about 26 million students in 90% of the country's school districts, according to the Bipartisan Policy Center.

Only students living in states that opt into the Education Freedom Tax Credit program would be eligible to receive scholarships. As of Oct. 22, 30 states have indicated that they will opt into the program, according to the IRS.

Participating states will approve scholarship-granting organizations to operate in their states, which will then direct taxpayer donations to families to use for education expenses.

ACE Scholarships is one such SGO. The Denver-based nonprofit is working with public and private school systems in several states interested in soliciting donations for the federal tax credit scholarships that can then be used at their schools, according to CEO Norton Rainey.

ACE Scholarships already has several charter school clients and is in talks with public school districts to manage and distribute their donation accounts, he says. The organization, he adds, will focus on directing scholarships to students with the greatest financial needs.

But while some are urging public school systems and supporters to embrace the federal scholarship program, critics have many concerns. They worry about a lack of accountability, a financial drain on public schools and potential inequities.

"Simply permitting some public school students to benefit does not ensure that resources are equitably distributed or that schools and students most in need are served by the program," according to a coalition of 16 civil rights advocacy organizations in a Sept. 28 letter to state governors.

The rapid increase in school choice options is leading more public school leaders to consider creative ways to invest in marketing to keep their enrollment numbers up, says Mellissa Braham, associate director of the National School Public Relations Association.

But that doesn’t mean they necessarily have the budgets to do so, Braham says.

“Because districts nationwide are facing really tough times right now, a lot of them are having to reduce staff, and in that light, it's painful to put additional focus on marketing, even though ultimately it does help your budget,” she says.

One way districts are approaching enrollment marketing in a small yet impactful way is by having dedicated in-house staff solicit student and teacher testimonials and create videos to post on their communication channels.

Other low-cost marketing strategies include paying to boost social media posts or have their school or district website appear on search engines more frequently instead of relying on organic search, Braham says. Some districts are paying for targeted marketing emails, too.

She points to successful elements she's seen in school marketing campaigns, including firsthand testimonials from students and families, a focus on what makes a school and district unique, and targeted emails that feel specific to the person receiving it.

But districts in highly competitive areas may need to invest in more costly marketing efforts, she says. That could include buying billboard, television or streaming ads.

“If you're in a really competitive environment with a lot of different schools to choose from, it might be an investment you have to make with the ultimate return of enrollment, which brings in funds, which allows you to support staff programs and services,” Braham says.

In districts where school choice options are fairly new, Braham says, sometimes the biggest challenge for school leaders is to understand that families are now customers and that neighborhood public schools are not the only option.

Shifting to a marketing mindset is different from sending out regular school communications, which aim to share information and build relationships, she says. And that change is “new for a lot of public schools.”

Lucid CEO says the company didn't know where its own cars were

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Gaps in its logistics system caused the EV maker to lose orders.

Lucid, the California-based Tesla rival that makes luxury EVs like the Air and Gravity, has an interesting excuse for its third quarter earnings underperformance. The company said it lost sales due to inventory control issues, with CEO Silvio Napoli admitting in an earnings call that sometimes "we did not even know where cars were." That chaos resulted in slow delivery times up to two weeks that caused Lucid to lose orders from impatient buyers.

The company doesn't have millions of cars to keep track of like Tesla, either. Lucid built just 2,954 vehicles last quarter and delivered 3,806, down seven percent from the year before. Building fewer cars than it sold helped it reduce inventory, but it still had $1.22 billion worth on the books at the end of September.

With its logistics issues, Lucid's sales suffered like Tesla's did when the latter started ramping up the Model 3. Elon Musk's company had to take drastic measures to solve supply issues (like building its own car carriers and buying trucking companies), but once it did, sales took off.

Lucid's Napoli suggested that its latest fixes to shipping problems will have a similar effect by allowing the commercial team to "sell the cars that we know can be available on the day for the customers who require it." With that, along with a growing US EV market due to high gas prices (plus a deal it recently cut with robotaxi company Bolt), the company has positive expectations for the latter half of 2026.

Fixing its supply chain won't solve Lucid's other problems, though, like quality control issues and customer service complaints. The other tiny issue is that the automaker lost $831 million last quarter on revenue of just $350 million, due to cost of making each vehicle that far exceeds the selling price. However, that didn't discourage the company's primary investor, the Saudi Public Investment Fund (PIF), from injecting an additional $1.75 billion into Lucid.

The 10 Affordable Cities Where Homeowners Are the Least Locked In by Housing Costs

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many homeowners who began the year hoping to move may now be locked in by soaring mortgage rates that threaten to nearly double their monthly payments—but a handful of budget-friendly markets still offer relief.

These rare pockets of affordability are clustered primarily across the Midwest and South, including Pittsburgh; Baltimore; Buffalo, NY; New Orleans; Detroit; Birmingham, AL; San Antonio, TX; Akron, OH; McAllen, TX; and Philadelphia.

Nationally, the typical U.S. mortgage holder pays roughly $1,300 in principal and interest per month. If they were to purchase a home today, that payment would surge by more than 85%, or $1,100, according to a new report on America's "locked-in" homeowners from Realtor.com®.

The market's current predicament can be traced to the COVID-19 pandemic years, when historically low mortgage rates whipped up a buying and refinancing frenzy. As of July 2026, more than a quarter (27%) of all fixed-rate mortgages on record for single-family homes and condominiums were originated in 2020 and 2021 alone.

Once mortgage rates began climbing again in 2022, homebuying and refinancing both hit a wall. Over the subsequent four years, only about 1 in 4 mortgages on record was originated, highlighting the sharp pullback in activity as a result of higher borrowing costs.

"For those who purchased before mortgage rates shot sharply higher, the sentiment of 'I couldn’t afford to buy my home today' continues to ring true in most cases, contributing to historically low mobility and some of the leanest for-sale inventory on record," notes Realtor.com senior economist Hannah Jones.

In other words, homeowners who were fortunate enough to secure a sub-4% mortgage rate at the height of the pandemic are reluctant to list. Trading up or downsizing today means taking out a mortgage with a steeply higher rate, gobbling up a much bigger share of their income.

Just how much bigger? Purchasing a median-priced home at the Oct. 22 rate of 7.32%, assuming a 14.3% down payment, would translate into a monthly principal and interest payment of nearly $2,500, almost double the $1,300 typical owners currently pay.

However, in parts of the U.S. where home prices remain accessible, the "lock-in" effect is felt less acutely, keeping inventory—and people—moving and creating a self-reinforcing cycle.

"The affordability of these markets enables mobility, which means the typical outstanding mortgage rate is higher, which helps reduce the payment jump associated with moving, which reinforces affordability," explains Jones.

In the South and Midwest, approximately three-quarters of mortgages carry a rate below 6%, compared to 78% nationally.

Additionally, nearly a quarter of all outstanding mortgages in these two regions were originated within the past four years at 6% or higher, compared with 21.6% in the Northeast and 17.7% in the West.

"That mix means fewer homeowners in these regions are clinging to 3%–4% rates, which lessens the lock-in effect," says Jones.

Across the 100 largest metros, Pittsburgh stands out as the nation's least "locked in" housing market, where a seller purchasing a new home in town would see a 43% increase in their monthly mortgage payment—a modest penalty compared to the 165% in San Jose, CA.

Baltimore is a close second, with a 45% payment gap, followed by Buffalo, at 46.1%.

"A 3% mortgage is a great reason to stay in a house, but it's not always a great reason to stay in a house that no longer fits your life," Colleen Collier, associate broker at Re/Max Plus in Buffalo, tells Realtor.com.

The latest housing data reflects the market's mobility and affordability. In October, Buffalo posted the largest year-over-year increase in active listings (+34.4%) among the 50 largest metros, paired with the largest growth in new listings (+13.1%).

At the same time, the median listing price in the metro registered at $259,999, down 2.8% from a year ago and sitting more than $155,000 below the national median.

Collier notes that while more local homeowners are willing to make a move, it does not necessarily mean they are comfortable giving up their low rates. They are being compelled by life changes.

"They're retiring, downsizing, or relocating to be closer to family," says the broker. "Many are looking to reduce maintenance and their overall living expenses. Others are relocating to a warmer climate. ... They're taking the equity they've built and putting it toward their next purchase."

Collier recently represented clients who sold their home of 30 years to downsize into an apartment. They were able to get a final sale price well above asking, which was enough to cover at least 10 years of apartment living, and fund future travel plans and a new car.

"What made this experience so rewarding was that the homeowner had more equity than they realized," says Collier. "After more than three decades in their home, selling wasn't simply about moving into a smaller space. It was about creating financial freedom and enjoying the next chapter of life."

In Akron, a typical homeowner currently paying just $868 per month should expect to see that figure rise nearly 62% under present-day market conditions—well above Buffalo’s level but still a far cry from the most locked-in coastal markets.

"The financial difference between staying in a home with a low mortgage rate and purchasing another home may be less dramatic here than in higher-priced markets," Mike Valerino, CEO of Akron Cleveland Association of Realtors, tells Realtor.com. "However, that doesn't mean the mortgage rate lock-in effect has disappeared."

According to Valerino, in Summit County, where Akron is located, new listings and closed sales were down in September while the average sales price increased, suggesting that despite the market's relative affordability, homeowners are still weighing the financial implications of moving.

"Life circumstances remain important drivers of housing decisions," he says, echoing Collier's argument. "Growing families need more space, empty nesters may want to downsize, and job changes, retirement, and family considerations can create reasons to move that outweigh the financial benefits of staying put."

At the same time, Valerino says that Akron's relative affordability does not insulate buyers and sellers from the challenges affecting housing markets nationally.

"The mortgage lock-in effect may be less severe here, but the cost of the next home still plays a major role in whether someone decides to move," he says.

In roughly a third of the 100 largest U.S. metros, a move to a median-priced home would more than double the monthly payment. Ballooning borrowing costs disproportionately penalize expensive coastal and fast-growing markets, leaving local homeowners feeling virtually trapped by their lower mortgages.

According to the latest data, the most "locked-in" markets in the U.S. include Portland, ME, San Jose, and Los Angeles, where anticipated monthly mortgage payments surged by more than 140%, grinding mobility to a halt. That friction has severely squeezed inventory and put upward pressure on home prices, sidelining first-time and budget buyers.

"Because homes in these markets are already expensive, buyers typically rely on larger mortgage balances, which amplifies the impact of rising rates," says Jones.

In Portland, for instance, a current owner holding on to a 4% mortgage rate makes a median payment of $1,270 per month. If they were to purchase a home comparable to theirs with a 26.2% down payment and took out a 7.32% mortgage, their payment would jump a staggering 167% to $3,392.

Mortgage rates would have to drop a long way from their current 7.18% level to close the gap between what current owners pay and what a buyer would pay today.

Rates would need to reach 6% before half of U.S. households could afford the median-priced home, and even then a move would still mean a payment about 62% higher than what the typical mortgage holder spends now.

Crucially, however, Jones stresses that falling mortgage rates alone are not enough. Because home values have appreciated significantly over the past few years, buying a median-priced home with a 4% mortgage would still cost about 29% more per month than what existing homeowners pay on their original COVID-19-era loans.

Valerino agrees, maintaining that there is no one "magic number" that will unlock the market.

"Rates in the 5% range could make moving more attractive to some homeowners, but affordability involves more than the interest rate," he says. "Home prices, property taxes, insurance, and accumulated equity all factor into that decision."

The market is poised to loosen gradually rather than all at once as more households buy in to higher rates, softening price growth narrows the gap, and rising incomes boost buying power.

"The lower-priced Midwest and South markets, where the gap is smallest and more loans carry higher rates, are likely to see the most activity first," predicts Jones. "Large coastal markets will need both lower rates and lower prices or significantly higher incomes before sellers feel free to move."

Snejana Farberov is a reporter at Realtor.com covering the U.S. housing market and the latest domestic real estate trends. She has worked as a general assignment journalist in New York City and Long Island for 16 years, writing for New York Post, Daily Mail, and News 12. Snejana earned bachelor's degrees in journalism and Italian from St. John's University, followed by a master’s degree from Columbia University School of Journalism.

Why Is Crypto Crashing Today? Bitcoin Falls Below $73K as Zcash (ZEC) Leads Crypto Losses

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Why is crypto crashing? A hot CPI report raised Fed hike bets. ZEC fell below $1,000, the worst of 20 large coins this week.

Why is crypto crashing on Wednesday, November 11? The sell-off that began with Tuesday’s hotter-than-expected U.S. inflation report has carried into today, and almost every major coin is lower.

Bitcoin (BTC) dropped below $73,000 on Tuesday, touching $72,727 on Binance, and now trades at about $73,800. Ether (ETH) briefly slipped to $2,006, while XRP and Solana (SOL) are down 2% to 3% in 24 hours. The Crypto Fear & Greed Index, a daily gauge of market mood from 0 to 100, has fallen to 29.

The report changed what traders expect from the Federal Reserve. Bond yields jumped, and bets on a larger December rate hike nearly tripled within two hours.

One coin is falling much harder than the rest. Zcash (ZEC) is a cryptocurrency launched in 2016 that lets users hide the sender, the receiver and the amount of a payment. It dropped below $1,000 on Wednesday for the first time since September 5. ZEC now trades at about $966, down 15.4% in 24 hours and 24.8% in a week.

ZEC has been one of this year’s best performers. It rose from about $526 on January 1 to a closing high of $1,653 on September 26. We checked the 20 largest coins on Binance, and ZEC fell the most over both the day and the week, even though it is still up 89% in 2026.

Trader 0x8742 opened a 10x long on 18,000 $ZEC ($17.63M) and is now down over $2.8M.

Liquidation price: $895.46.https://t.co/azzt3xvmay pic.twitter.com/wczndt7doC

— Lookonchain (@lookonchain) November 11, 2026

So why are the markets spooked, and why is ZEC taking the hardest hit?

Inflation came in hot. The consumer price index (CPI) rose 0.4% in October, above the 0.3% economists expected. Core prices, which leave out food and energy, rose 0.3% against a 0.2% forecast.

That reading crossed a line the Fed had drawn. Fed Governor Christopher Waller said in a speech hours before the report that core inflation of about 0.3% would make a half-point hike in December worth considering.

Traders took him at his word. On Polymarket, the odds of a hike of 0.5 percentage point or more in December went from 4.4% just before the report to 12.8% two hours later. They sit at 9.4% now, while a standard quarter-point hike is priced at 85.5%.

Higher rates hurt crypto in a simple way. They make cash and bonds pay more, so money flows out of riskier bets. On Tuesday, the 10-year U.S. Treasury yield rose from 4.86% to 4.98%.

Market Intelligence: Crypto Analyst Predicts Best Low-Cap Crypto

ZEC had the most to give back. We compared the price changes of 20 large coins on Binance over the past week and since January 1.

When markets get nervous, traders tend to sell what has made them the most money. ZEC has fallen 41% from its September 26 closing high of $1,653.

Borrowed money made the drop faster. On Hyperliquid, ZEC fell from $1,199.20 to $965.79 in less than a day. Lookonchain counted 32 traders holding more than $1 million each in leveraged bets on a rising ZEC price. Of those, 27 are now losing money, with about $23.7 million in paper losses between them.

Sharp drops have not always lasted. Since January 2025, ZEC has fallen 12% or more in a single day 14 times on Binance. Seven days later, it was higher eight times, with a median gain of 6.5%.

EXPLORE: Fed Meeting Today: Traders Price a 95% Hold and a 79% December Hike. What Does It Mean for Crypto?

ZEC trades at about $966. It touched $961.07 on Wednesday, its lowest price since September 4.

On the daily chart, where each candle covers one day, ZEC has fallen below its 50-day exponential moving average (EMA), an average that gives recent prices more weight, at $1,200.68. It is still above its 200-day EMA at $872.23.

For Bitcoin, the line to watch is Tuesday’s low of $72,727. Below that, $68,902, the August 20 low, is the next support.

So, why is crypto crashing? A hot inflation report raised the odds of a bigger Fed hike, and ZEC’s leveraged bulls are paying the most for it. A daily close back above $1,050 would be the first sign the selling is easing.

West Ham interested in 22-year-old goalkeeper with minimum $15m transfer fee demanded

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

West Ham have encountered a goalkeeping dilemma, with Mads Hermansen struggling for form and Alphonse Areola now injured. Attention has turned to the transfer market and potential solutions, and according to Argentine news outlet TyC Sports, WHUFC have eyes on River Plate’s Santiago Beltran.

It is claimed that the Hammers are among the clubs to have shown interest in the 22-year-old, but Spanish giants Atletico Madrid and Premier League side Aston Villa also have eyes on the goalkeeper. River Plate have been keen to extend Beltran’s contract, which expires in 2027, but they are willing to sell if $15m (£11.2m) is tabled.

The Argentine club would be willing to sell in January too, which will suit the Hammers as they look to resolve their goalkeeping issues. Alongside Hermansen and Areola, academy graduate Finlay Herrick is an option available to new boss Scott Parker.

Beltran is a product of the River Plate academy and has broken into the first team setup over the past 18 months. He made his international debut for Argentina in the summer but is also eligible to represent Italy.

Over the course of 2026, Beltran has locked down the starting berth with one of his country’s most prestigious sides. Given that his contract runs out at the end of 2027, it is little wonder that interest from West Ham, Atletico Madrid and Aston Villa is emerging.

In Liga Professional, Argentina’s top flight, Beltran has recorded 50 saves and an xG prevented of 3.93. It shows that while he isn’t necessarily highly active in terms of shot-stopping behind a tight defence, he is doing a lot of individual work to deny goal-bound shots when he is called upon.

He is solid in terms of his short-distance distribution with 259 successful passes, but he has a long ball accuracy of only 27.8%. This is an area of his game to improve, but at only 22, the WHUFC target has plenty of time to grow in this area (all stats via FotMob).

Beltran’s success in Argentina indicates he would be an upgrade on West Ham’s current options. Interest from Atletico Madrid and Aston Villa emphasises the high regard he is held in too.

Importantly, Beltran’s age means that he could become the Hammers’ long-term No. 1. He has both time and room to grow, mainly in his distribution, so he can become an important player off the bat while growing into a valuable financial asset in the years to come.

At around £11m, he wouldn’t be a cheap signing. But, at that price, WHUFC would be signing a goalkeeper upgrade who can only grow in value as he progresses. Beltran feels like a good target and a smart option in the effort to tighten up in goal.

PS5 Has Officially Crossed 100 Million Sales, Sony Confirms

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The PS5 has now officially surpassed the 100 million sales milestone.

This news comes via Sony itself, which has just released newly updated figures for the current-gen console.

On the Sony Interactive Entertainment website, it states PS5 has sold "more than 100 million" units as of 10th November, 2026.

This means that, since the end of the last financial quarter (30th September 2026), Sony has shifted another 2.1 million machines.

It's taken slightly longer for PS5 to reach 100 million than its predecessor, the PS4, which was reportedly the fastest ever console to hit the milestone.

Given the many hurdles PS5 has contended with during its lifespan — launching amid the COVID-19 pandemic, shortages, hardware prices skyrocketing, etc. — it's quite remarkable it's managed to maintain such a healthy momentum.

It's possible this recent surge in PS5 sales is related to the imminent arrival of GTA 6, as well as the usual bump due to the approaching holiday season.

In any case, PS5 joins the list of consoles that have surpassed the milestone, and it's the fourth Sony home console to achieve it.

Sony also reports that PS5 is its most profitable generation ever, so all-in-all, it's been a very good generation for business.

The console still has a bit of time left before the inevitable PS6, which is expected to arrive within the next couple of years; whether Sony can keep the momentum going into the new generation remains to be seen.
