A new tactic in the war over out-of-network bills: Health care providers are suing employers

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Herman examined hundreds of pages of court documents for this story, part of STAT’s Out of Pocket, Out of Reach series.

Bob covers the money in health care, focusing on health insurance and hospitals. His stories delve into Medicare Advantage, opaque prescription drug benefits, and how much executives actually make. He is also the author of the Health Care Inc. newsletter. You can reach Bob on Signal at bobjherman.09.

This past August, Joe Hancock, a Kentucky plumbing and heating contractor, opened a just-delivered box packed with hundreds of sheets of paper. Leafing through them, he read seven words no small business owner wants to see: “A lawsuit has been filed against you.”

The complaint, from air ambulance firm PHI Health, claimed Hancock was on the hook for almost $27,000 in medical helicopter bills. They were tied to one of his now-retired employees, who needed an emergency flight after a heart attack.

Hancock, who owns Whitehead-Hancock Plumbing, Heating & Cooling, a family-run business in Frankfort, Ky., that has been around for more than a century, didn’t understand why he was being roped into a lawsuit that featured 31 other companies — for medical charges he was never aware of.

“I was terrified,” Hancock said. “I was like, ‘What in the hell is going on here?’”

He’s not alone. The PHI lawsuit highlights an evolving tactic by health care providers in a raging battle over out-of-network bills. Previously, providers targeted health insurance companies to pay outstanding amounts that were awarded through federal mediation. Now, they increasingly are dragging the insurers’ clients — employers — into court, claiming they are responsible for bills that insurers haven’t fully paid or have delayed paying. The practice is introducing a new shock that could further torch the affordability of workers’ health coverage.

“These big suits naming dozens of employers at a time is really an escalation,” said Garrett Hohimer, a vice president at the Business Group on Health. “And it will cause employers to incur expenses that [come directly] out of their health care plans.”

A 2022 law called the No Surprises Act shielded patients from the financial trauma of many types of unexpected medical bills, but it has also given rise to a vast industry that’s driving up costs across the health care system. The law uses an arbitration process to determine how much health care providers should get paid for out-of-network emergency care, like helicopter rides, for people who have some type of commercial health coverage. STAT has reported that providers are winning most of these arbitration awards, which often are far higher than the amounts in-network providers receive for the same services.

Already, high payments tied to this process have added approximately $20 billion in extra costs to the health care system, Georgetown University researchers have estimated. A large portion of those costs inevitably come out of the bank accounts of America’s employers and consequently lead to higher premiums, emphasizing how even well-intentioned health reforms can add to the financial pressure that’s crushing workers and businesses.

PHI Health, a national chain, is arguably the most aggressive company suing employers, which it claims are not “passive” bystanders in insurers’ refusal to fully pay awards. Other regional physician practices have sued some employers as well. But PHI and its army of attorneys have filed lawsuits against hundreds of companies, demanding they collectively pay tens of millions of dollars in unpaid out-of-network claims that it won in the federal arbitration process.

PHI isn’t just suing small, local firms like Hancock’s. It’s also taking legal action against some of the largest corporations — Amazon, Walmart, Eli Lilly, United Airlines, General Motors, PepsiCo, State Farm, and Toyota, to name a few — as well as unions, counties, cities, states, public school districts, and universities.

PHI has sued other health care organizations, including hospitals, nursing homes, and medical billing firms. It also has gone after nonprofits, such as the Catholic Diocese of Cleveland. In each instance, a worker or family member at these companies took an emergency medical helicopter, often at a cost of tens of thousands of dollars per trip.

Unpacking the business — and secretive inner workings — of the U.S. health care industry

In some cases, PHI wasn’t sure what company it should be suing, but sued anyway. Several lawsuits named specific health insurers and employers as defendants, and then added “ABC CORP” or “XYZ CORP” as a placeholder. If the insurer or employer “is not in fact the plan sponsor … that covers the patient transported by PHI, then the identity of the plan sponsor is unknown to PHI, and this action is instead brought against ABC CORP,” one lawsuit reads.

Katie Keith and Ellie DeGarmo are attorneys at Georgetown’s Center for Health Policy and the Law who have tracked No Surprises Act lawsuits, including those filed by PHI. They said the scores of cases reinforce the bloat in the health care system: above-market prices that providers are winning for not participating in an insurance company’s network, attempts from insurers to not pay at all or profit off lowball offers, and a booming revenue stream for arbitrators.

But trying to wring money out of employers — and, ultimately, from their workers — is a stark divergence from the public statements of physician groups and air ambulance companies, which have painted health insurers as the bad guys for not following the rules.

“It kind of undercuts the narrative that this is provider versus insurance company,” Keith said. “In this case, this is about provider versus everyone who pays for health care and is now stuck with the bill for [arbitration] awards that have gone totally off the rails.”

STAT reached out to more than two dozen employers that were listed in PHI’s lawsuits. Most did not respond to inquiries. Of those that did, most declined to comment.

“Because this matter involves pending litigation … we will not be providing any interviews,” said Clint Mitchell, the division superintendent of Spotsylvania County Public Schools in Fredericksburg, Va. PHI Health is suing Spotsylvania County’s school system for almost $28,000.

Jay Litten, the town manager of Bridgewater, Va., told STAT the town’s health insurer, Anthem Blue Cross Blue Shield, retained counsel and was handling the case. PHI sued Bridgewater in October for more than $37,000 in unpaid awards.

PHI has boasted about its success in the law’s arbitration process. In 2024, it told prospective investors that it was “able to maintain our profitability” by winning more than three out of every four arbitration claims, and it expected “this trend of favorable” outcomes from the process would continue.

The company has some private equity backing, according to financial filings from when the company considered going public in 2023. Its largest shareholder as of 2023 was Q Investments, a family office that has a private equity firm.

PHI declined to make anyone available for an interview. In a statement, CEO Scott McCarty defended the company’s lawsuits, saying the employers PHI has sued “have a duty to their employees and a responsibility to health care providers in their communities to abide by the law and pay for services delivered.”

McCarty said health insurers are asking their employer clients to choose between paying the unpaid arbitration amounts or authorizing the insurer to continue refusing payment.

“Once an employer is on notice of nonpayment and affirmatively endorses that practice, it is no longer a passive bystander,” McCarty said in the statement. “It has made a conscious decision to allow its insurance company not to pay binding federal awards in violation of federal law.”

In PHI’s lawsuits, the company alleges insurers, and now employers, are using an “unlawful scheme to ‘low pay, late pay, or no pay’ providers, including PHI.” All the cases are still pending.

The company also claims insurers float the money from unpaid awards, which are binding decisions, on the stock market as a way to earn extra interest or investment income.

James Gelfand, CEO of the ERISA Industry Committee, a lobbying group that represents large employers, said companies don’t condone insurers delaying payments as a way to make extra money. But he said under the federal law known as ERISA, employers are legally obligated to make sure they spend their health plan assets wisely on behalf of workers. If employers believe the awards are tied to ineligible claims — or are egregiously high compared with what they normally would pay — those payments could violate that obligation.

As a result, the employers’ health insurers “are operating with our blessing when we say, ‘Hey, if the [arbitration] award is inappropriate and wrong, do not pay it,’” he said. “How can we claim to be responsible stewards of people’s money if we’re just like, ‘Oh, here you go!’ That would be crazy.”

Jenny Goins, who is CEO of the National Alliance of Healthcare Purchaser Coalitions and used to oversee health benefits for Kentucky state employees, said “slow-walking the payments” to providers is one of the only ways employers can push back if they believe the awards are based on ineligible or incomplete claims. Medical claims are not eligible for arbitration if, for example, the employer or its health plan doesn’t cover a certain service or if there is certain information or data missing.

PHI has frequently named Anthem Blue Cross Blue Shield, owned by Elevance Health, as a defendant in the lawsuits against employers. Elevance did not make anyone available for an interview. The company said in a statement that PHI is charging prices for air ambulance services that are 700% of what Medicare pays and mileage rates that are 1,500% more than Medicare rates.

“We have already paid PHI substantial amounts for services provided to our members,” Elevance spokesperson Janey Kiryluik said in a statement. “We dispute whether additional amounts are legally owed for claims that were ineligible” for the arbitration process.

PHI said only arbitrators can determine the eligibility of claims. It added that the rates Medicare pays don’t cover the full cost of emergency air care, nor can those prices be considered during the arbitration process.

The bigger issue revealed by the lawsuits, Goins said, is how “broken” the law has become, even though she believed it was well-intended. The issue has now attracted interest in Congress, where lawmakers are proposing various plans to overhaul the arbitration process.

“Greedy people see an opportunity to make more money,” Goins said. “I also see there’s a true opportunity to shine a light on this.”

In September, PHI dropped Hancock’s company as a defendant, one month after its attorneys served him with court papers. Hancock said his heating, cooling, and plumbing business of 30 employees is not self-insured — meaning he buys his health insurance from Anthem, which takes on all the risk of his employees’ medical care.

PHI’s McCarty blamed Anthem in a statement, saying it “does not always receive clear or consistent information from insurers identifying whether a plan is self-funded or fully insured. In this case, after the lawsuit was filed, Anthem’s counsel confirmed that Whitehead-Hancock was fully insured, and PHI promptly dismissed it, without settlement.”

Other businesses are getting released from PHI’s lawsuits, too. It’s unclear if they were wrongfully lumped in, like Hancock’s company, or if they are paying PHI’s demands to clear a costly legal headache.

PHI’s legal team “threw a handful of darts, and they’re going to try to rattle as many cages as they can,” Hancock said. “It’s not right. It shouldn’t be legal.”

STAT’s coverage of the soaring costs of health insurance for workers and employers is supported by a grant from the Commonwealth Fund. Our financial supporters are not involved in any decisions about our journalism.

Bob covers the money in health care, focusing on health insurance and hospitals. His stories delve into Medicare Advantage, opaque prescription drug benefits, and how much executives actually make. He is also the author of the Health Care Inc. newsletter. You can reach Bob on Signal at bobjherman.09.

Malaysia Airlines Overtakes AirAsia on India Routes by Standing Still

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

AirAsia’s fuel-driven retreat should be a gift to its rivals, but high fuel costs and scarce aircraft mean few can afford to take advantage. Malaysia Airlines has overtaken it on India routes with 1% more seats in a market that shrank 10%. The longer AirAsia stays small, the more of those default gains could become permanent.

AirAsia is shrinking across Southeast Asia, and the clearest sign of what that means for rivals is on routes between Malaysia and India.

For the first time since 2021, the Malaysia-based low-cost airline group is no longer the biggest operator  between Malaysia and India. Malaysia Airlines has scheduled 1.57 million seats on the route in 2026, against a combined 1.42 million for AirAsia and its long-haul affiliate AirAsia X, according to Cirium data.

Malaysia Airlines got there without growing much. Its seats are up 1% from 2025. The AirAsia group's are meanwhile down 21% — a loss of nearly 378,000 seats in a year.

The overall Malaysia–India market has shrunk 10% in seats this year. Malaysia Airlines' share has climbed from 34% to 38%, while AirAsia group’s share has fallen from 39% to 35%. Some rivals are not filling the gap: IndiGo's seats fell 18% and Batik Air's 9%. Air India is the only carrier that’s growing strongly — it i

Skift provides the insights. You make the decisions.

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

AirAsia has lost its position as the largest operator between Malaysia and India for the first time since 2021, a measurable marker of the low-cost group's retrenchment across Southeast Asia. Malaysia Airlines has scheduled 1.57 million seats on the route in 2026 against 1.42 million for AirAsia and AirAsia X combined, and it got there with just 1% growth while the AirAsia group cut nearly 378,000 seats, down 21%. Fuel is the main cause: jet fuel averaged $183 a barrel in the second quarter, up 66% from the prior quarter, and the largely unhedged group posted a net loss of RM831 million ($204 million). AirAsia has suspended 81 routes, plans to return 25 aircraft to lessors early, and is seeking to raise up to $1 billion mainly to refinance debt. Rivals are collecting share by default rather than chasing it — Batik Air's Malaysia–India seats are down 9% despite telling Malaysia's government it could absorb AirAsia's routes, and only Air India is growing strongly, up 29%. With narrowbody delivery slots scarce until roughly 2033, nobody can add capacity quickly without someone else's planes, and analysts quoted by Skift said handing back jets now could slow AirAsia's eventual comeback.

PlayStation's embattled shooter Fairgames has finally been cancelled, a new report says, as live service push withers away

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

After years of silence, we finally know the fate of PlayStation's embattled live service game Fairgames. The Haven-developed co-op heist shooter has been cancelled, with the silver lining that the studio is not closing down or laying off staff.

According to a new Bloomberg report, the decision to cancel Fairgames was made following the results of a recent playtest. Sony told staff on Tuesday of the playtest's poor reception and of its decision to end development.

"While the test demonstrated strong operational excellence, live ops readiness and community engagement, player feedback and retention results were mixed and fell short of the goals we had set," executives Sean Decker and John Doyle reportedly wrote in an email to staff.

"Combined with challenging market conditions in the PvP shooter genre, we have made the difficult decision to cease development on the game."

A PlayStation spokesperson later confirmed that developers at Haven will now support other teams within the PlayStation Studios group, as they continue to "explore new creative opportunities".

Fairgames was initially announced in 2023 as one of several in-development live service games, as part of an initiative by former PlayStation boss Jim Ryan to create more than a dozen games that generate recurring revenue.

Most of those titles were cancelled before they were even revealed, and those that made it out, such as Concord, flopped severely. Even Sony darling Guerrilla Games seemingly can't make it work, with recent reports saying the studio's Horizon Hunters Gathering - initially envisioned as a live service spin on the robo dinosaur series - will now shift into a more traditional co-op structure without live service elements. That's assuming the team can show a revamped version of the game to executives by December.

The news of Fairgames' cancellation isn't surprising. The game has had a tumultuous development journey ever since its reveal. Initially set for release in 2025, the game was soon pushed back to 2026, following the departure of studio founder Jade Raymond.

Though Sony later reiterated its commitment to its remaining in-development live service titles, Fairgames was never shown to the public again. That same year, game director Daniel Drapeau left Haven - another key departure suggesting the game had difficulties and might not see release.

Throughout that time, Fairgames has reportedly been reworked multiple times, with a plan for it to be re-revealed under a new name. That name is believed to be Break In, linked to a trademark Sony filed in May, but with the project cancelled, we may never know if that was the case.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Roth Conversion - Using withholding method

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →


They surely are familiar with that. Trouble is, they're mostly unfamiliar with the fact that this is a special type of indirect rollover officially termed a "conversion". To get the 5498 done correctly, you need to be explicit and make that very clear.
So say "indirect rollover" and "Roth Conversion", but do not mention
"make up the amount withheld with funds from other sources" ?
https://www.irs.gov/publications/p590a# ... 1000230615
Saying "indirect rollover" is an invitation for them to code it as a generic (non-conversion) rollover, so don't say it. There's no need to. You could say: "I withdrew some cash from a traditional IRA within the last 60 days, and I would like to do a Roth conversion of that amount." That's completely truthful, it's a simple and straightforward transaction that they're probably familiar with, and it's all they need to know. Embellishing this with additional details can only work against you.

If you start talking about things like "make up the amount withheld with funds from other sources", or even mention the word "withholding", what seems to happen again and again is that the agent becomes confused about what you're doing, and you can't get it done.

You should understand (but don't try to get the agent to understand... they're probably not open to learning from you) that you are not converting the withholding. You are converting the amount withdrawn from the IRA. The fact that the withdrawn funds were used for withholding is irrelevant to this transaction.

Statistics: Posted by ssel — Wed Nov 11, 2026 3:28 am

Comedian Lucy Beaumont Fears Being Blacklisted By TV Industry After Blowing Whistle On David Walliams

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Comedian Lucy Beaumont has said she has risked her television career by speaking out about David Walliams because the industry doesn’t tolerate troublemakers.

In an interview with Sky News (watch below), the Taskmaster star and Hullraisers writer revealed that she was one of the “whistleblowers” on Walliams, who has been accused of using his fame and power to prey on teenage girls. Walliams, known for Little Britain and Doctor Who, strongly denies wrongdoing.

Beaumont told The Cathy Newman Show that a young person working on a TV show approached her with concerns that Walliams allegedly hosted teenagers in his dressing room. Beaumont said she contacted one of the young girls. Related Stories News David Walliams Being Cut From New Movie 'Fing!' Following Allegations; Comcast-Owned Sky Exits UK Distribution Of Film News 'Britain's Got Talent' Producer Urges "Anyone Who Feels They Have Been Subject Of Wrongdoing" To Come Forward Following David Walliams Allegations

“She was in a really bad way,” Beaumont said. “I was really worried she was going to take her own life because of it. She had put stuff online, and there was evidence that she was in a dressing room. I offered to pay legal fees to try and get her some protection — and I’m not saying necessarily that what she said was true — but I wanted to help her because it was a world that she was really out of her depth with.” Watch on Deadline

Beaumont continued: “I believe she had signed an NDA [non-disclosure agreement] to say — and this is the issue with some of these NDAs — the NDA wasn’t just about she couldn’t talk to the media. It was that she couldn’t — I’m nearly crying telling you this — talk to family or friends or a therapist.”

She added: “There’s no proper duty of care. There’s no social services that you deal with, and it’s partly because the industry is very well-managed.”

Turning to her decision to speak out, Beaumont said she doesn’t think she will work in television again because people “lose work” for challenging high-profile individuals and are painted as a “trouble causer.” She added that people are “very replaceable” in the business and there are “lots of other people who can do your job.” Beaumont joked that she would become a Pilates teacher.

ITV News and The Guardian published their investigation into Walliams on Tuesday, detailing how he allegedly used his position on shows like Little Britain and Britain’s Got Talent to meet teenagers and pursue sexual contact with them. One of the teenagers was alleged to be 15 at the time of her interaction with Walliams. Beaumont said she spoke to The Guardian as part of its reporting.

“I am not a sexual predator… I have not broken the law,” Walliams told ITV News. “I am devastated that some of the women I met ten to twenty years ago have now expressed hurt or anger towards me.”

He continued: “Looking back, I realise that at times I behaved thoughtlessly towards other people I had casual sexual relationships with… I realise I did not always take people’s feelings properly into account. That is something I regret. And whilst a power imbalance is inevitable when one party is famous, abusing that power is something else entirely and it is something that I never did.”

American soldier arrested on suspicion of spying for Russia

📅 2026-11-11  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A senior soldier in the U.S. Army has been arrested in the U.K. on suspicion of spying for Russia, London's Metropolitan Police said on Tuesday evening.

The unnamed 41-year-old man was arrested near Gloucester, a city in western England, on Tuesday as part of an investigation by the U.K.'s Counter Terrorism Policing. British police worked with U.S. authorities, including the military and the FBI, in their investigations.

A senior NATO military official confirmed on Tuesday that the American service member was assigned to the military alliance's Allied Rapid Reaction Corps (ARRC).

ARRC, headquartered in the English county of Gloucestershire, is a  3-star NATO headquarters with more than 400 permanent staff from more than 20 nations ready to deploy land forces where they are needed.

Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov told reporters on Wednesday that Moscow had "absolutely nothing to do with" the case in the U.K.

The individual at the center of the case, detained in London, is suspected of committing offenses under Sections 1 and 3 of the U.K.'s National Security Act.

Section 1 offenses relate to obtaining or disclosing protected information, while Section 3 involves assisting a foreign intelligence service.

The maximum penalty is life imprisonment for a Section 1 offense and 14 years' imprisonment for a Section 3 offense. A fine may also be imposed for either offense.

Police officers are carrying out searches at two locations in Gloucestershire, the Metropolitan Police added.

The arrest comes as Washington is seeking to broker an end to Russia's war in Ukraine. U.S.-mediated talks between Russian and Ukrainian officials began in Abu Dhabi on Tuesday, the first such meeting since February, while Kyiv and its European allies continue to press for credible security guarantees as part of any settlement.

President Donald Trump, a longstanding critic of NATO and its members' defense budgets, said on Monday that there appeared to be a growing willingness on both sides to end the almost five-year war.

Russia has long threatened retaliatory action against an expansion of NATO's eastern flank and has previously demanded guarantees that Ukraine will never be allowed to join the alliance. Finland and Sweden became NATO's newest members in 2023 and 2024, respectively.

Since Russia and Ukraine have been at war, Russian activity has also been reported deeper into European and NATO territory, with regional authorities investigating whether Moscow could be involved in some of the recent disarray seen across the continent.

FBI Director Kash Patel said in a statement on Tuesday evening that the allegations against a U.S. serviceman were "of the utmost concern to the United States and our allies."

"I want to send a clear message – this FBI has no tolerance for anyone who breaks their oath to the United States and whose actions endanger our national security and that of our partners," he said.

Sean Stinchon, the commanding general of U.S. Army Counterintelligence Command, said in a statement that "the alleged conduct of a senior solider, entrusted with sensitive information, is a sobering reminder that insider threats are real and persistent."

Authorities said Tuesday that the investigation is not related to any other ongoing Counter Terrorism Policing investigations.

On Oct. 27, British prosecutors said they had charged a man with assisting a foreign intelligence service over an incident at a military base used by the U.S. for strikes on Iran. The base in question, RAF Fairford, is in Gloucestershire, around 30 miles from ARRC's headquarters at Imjin Barracks.

'NCIS' Star Katrina Law Warns the Entire Team Will Break in Season 24

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Amanda M. Castro is a Network TV writer at Collider and a New York–based journalist whose work has appeared in Newsweek, where she is an Associate Editor, and The U.S. Sun, where she previously served as a Senior Consumer Reporter.

She specializes in network television coverage, delivering sharp, thoughtful analysis of long-running procedural hits and ambitious new dramas across broadcast TV. At Collider, Amanda explores character arcs, storytelling trends, and the cultural impact of network series that keep audiences tuning in week after week.

Born and raised in Puerto Rico, Amanda is bilingual and holds a degree in Communication, Film, and Media Studies from the University of New Haven.

Editor's Note: The following contains spoilers from NCIS Season 24, Episode 3. NCIS Season 24 has given longtime fans a reason to celebrate Michael Weatherly's return as Tony DiNozzo, but the team won't ride that nostalgia wave forever. According to Katrina Law, the job's emotional toll is about to catch up with Jessica Knight and her colleagues in ways viewers haven't seen before. The actress, who has played Knight since Season 18, tells Collider that the team will face a breaking point this season, with some particularly revealing episodes still ahead.

"I think this season you really get to see a lot of Knight's vulnerability in a way that you've never experienced before," Law says. "This season, you get to see her break in a way that you haven't seen before." And Knight won't be the only one struggling. Law adds that viewers will see "the entire team break" in a way the series hasn't explored before, making the aftermath as important as the moments that lead to it.

Before those emotional storylines unfold, however, the November 10 episode, "Speechless," gives Law a very different reason to remember Season 24. The installment marks the acting debut of her seven-year-old daughter, Kinley Andreen, who plays a young girl caught in the middle of a frightening investigation. It's a family milestone for the actress, but it also brings a personal connection to an episode built around the lengths a parent will go to protect their child.

Knight has spent much of her time on NCIS proving she can handle whatever comes through the doors at headquarters. A former member of the agency's REACT team, she joined the Major Case Response Team after the deaths of several colleagues and the arrest of her former team leader. She's since become an established part of the group, but Law's comments suggest Season 24 will ask something different of her: What happens when the agent who used to hold it together can no longer do so?

Knight has already experienced significant personal and professional upheaval throughout her time on the series. Law's tease, though, points to a more exposed side of the character, one that hasn't necessarily had the same space to emerge amid the team's investigations. The actress is particularly interested in seeing audiences connect with that vulnerability, especially as the other agents face their own emotional struggles.

Law says two upcoming episodes have already been filmed, and she credits writer Scott Williams with understanding how to bring out the characters' emotional depths. "I think Scott Williams does such justice to the characters when it comes to their emotional depths and bringing out the best and the worst in them," she tells Collider. For Law, what makes the story worth watching is how the team comes together and moves forward after reaching that breaking point.

Tonight's episode, "Speechless," gives Law a chance to share the screen with someone she knows better than anyone: her daughter, Kinley. The seven-year-old makes her acting debut as Chloe Sims, an eight-year-old girl the NCIS team discovers hiding alone in her apartment after her Marine mother disappears.

Chloe is frightened and initially reluctant to help, but she has a surprising skill that could give investigators the breakthrough they need. Her fluency in Mandarin becomes an important part of the case, creating an unexpected connection between the young witness and Knight. Law tells Collider that the character's appearance makes that ability all the more striking, describing Chloe as a blonde-haired, green-eyed girl whose talent will surprise viewers.

The casting also makes the episode's central themes hit a little closer to home. The investigation revolves around a missing mother and a child left to navigate a frightening situation, while Law is experiencing the unusual privilege of playing opposite her own daughter. She says watching Kinley move from being her little girl to a young actor stepping into character was unlike anything she'd experienced.

Law's behind-the-scenes experience was just as memorable as what audiences will see on screen. In her Collider interview, she recalls watching Kinley switch into character, suddenly becoming Chloe rather than the little girl she knows at home. "I would literally kind of see my daughter disappear and then this little actress pop up," Law tells Collider. "It was surreal, but then I would also kind of see my daughter peeking through, looking for the validation of like, 'Mommy, am I doing a good job?'"

A great detective is really just a personality with a method attached, which is why we keep remaking the same four of them. Here is yours, what it says about you, and the case for them being the best in the business.

You let people think you are not a threat and then you simply never go away. Columbo is the only major detective show that tells you who did it in the first ten minutes, because the pleasure was never the puzzle, it was watching a rich man slowly realise he is in trouble.

You are the most interesting person in the room and you know exactly how to use it. Rian Johnson built Blanc as a gentleman detective who is also an outsider everywhere he goes, which lets him be charming at people who would never otherwise talk to him.

Everybody tells you everything, and you have never once asked them to. Jessica Fletcher is a crime novelist from a small town in Maine who is so warm and so obviously trustworthy that suspects confess to her essentially as a social courtesy.

You expect to be lied to and you are almost never disappointed. Chandler wrote Marlowe as a man with a rigid private code in a city that has none, which is why he takes the case, solves it, gets nothing out of it and takes the next one.

Kinley's Mandarin-speaking abilities also connect to Law's family. The actress, who is half Chinese and speaks fluent Mandarin, explains that her mother has been instrumental in passing the language down to the next generation. "Pretty much every day my mom will sit down with Kinley and go over her Mandarin skills and teach her," Law says. "So it's been passed down through generations to Kinley, and she's upholding her family traditions."

One challenge Law hadn't quite anticipated, though, was balancing all her responsibilities on set. She was Kinley's mother, acting coach, scene partner, and fellow performer at the same time. At one point, she tells Collider, she even had to remind her daughter to look at her rather than directly into the camera. Kinley eventually pointed out that she was looking at her mother, prompting a laugh from Law.

The actress also explained why allowing Kinley to act on NCIS doesn't mean she's changing her approach to protecting her daughter. Law has deliberately kept Kinley off social media, but the young girl wanted to take part in the show and was already familiar with its cast and crew after years of visiting the set. With her mother beside her throughout filming, surrounded by people she already knew and trusted, she says it was an especially comfortable introduction to acting.

Of course, Kinley's debut isn't the only major development adding extra excitement to Season 24. Weatherly is back as Tony DiNozzo, and Law is just as enthusiastic about his return as longtime viewers. An original NCIS fan herself, the actress describes the decision to bring Weatherly back as a brilliant move by the network.

She didn't expect Jessica to get involved in Tony's return. Law pitched several ideas for how Knight might interact with Weatherly's character, but never expected Ziva David (Cote de Pablo) to pull her into their history by asking her to keep tabs on him.

"I kind of love it," Law tells Collider, explaining that the unexpected setup creates a fun relationship between Knight and Weatherly. It's an interesting position for Jessica, who now finds herself caught up in a dynamic that predates her time on the team.

Law has a few ideas about where that connection could go. When asked what she'd like to see for Knight this season, she didn't hesitate: she wants Jessica and Ziva to finally share the screen, preferably in an action sequence or an interaction that leaves Tony thoroughly uncomfortable.

For a franchise that's been on television for more than two decades, it has found new ways to bring its characters together while making room for the next generation. Season 24 is doing both, giving viewers the nostalgia of Tony's return while promising a much more vulnerable side of the agents they've spent years following.

"Speechless" offers a particularly personal entry point into that larger story, but Law's comments suggest the emotional challenges ahead won't be limited to one case. With two more character-focused episodes already in the can, Knight and her teammates could be heading into some of their most difficult moments yet. And if Law gets her way, perhaps a certain former Mossad agent will be around to make things even more interesting.

The NCIS Season 24, Episode 5, "Speechless," airs Tuesday, November 10, 2026, at 8 p.m. ET / 7 p.m. CT on CBS.

7 Netflix Shows That Are Perfect From Beginning to End

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Chris is a UK-based film and TV writer, editor and occasional filmmaker. For more than a decade, he has written news, reviews and features covering movies, television and pop culture. He has also made short films and worked in video production, giving him experience on both sides of the screen. Chris runs the We Love Movies More Than You TikTok channel and has a particular interest in overlooked and short-lived TV shows.

When Netflix first revealed it was going to make original TV shows and movies, the rest of the industry was doubtful that the company that used to mail DVDs in the post would pose any real threat to the established network structure. Now 15 years on, we’re drowning in streaming services making TV shows at a rate that’s almost impossible to keep up with.

In that time, Netflix has had some serious hits, plenty of misses, and an alarming habit of canceling TV shows far too soon. While some of their biggest originals couldn’t stick the landing, all the shows on this list started strong and ended in a satisfying way, even though two were canceled before they could reach their planned endgames. To keep this list from being largely populated by Mike Flanagan and adaptations of Harlan Coben books, you’ll find no miniseries here. From the return of The Karate Kid to one of the best sci-fi series of the 21st century, these Netflix TV shows are all perfect from beginning to end.

A belated sequel series to the 1980s classic The Karate Kid didn’t have to be this enjoyably heartfelt or this good. A middle-aged Johnny Lawrence (William Zabka) finds himself broke and at a low point, but he finds renewed purpose when he meets bullied teenager Miguel (Xolo Maridueña). Reopening his old karate dojo reawakens a decades-old feud with former rival and successful business owner Daniel LaRusso (Ralph Macchio). Over the six seasons, Johnny and Daniel have more than one rematch, steadily become friends, and begrudgingly train the next generation of champions together.

Cobra Kai rarely takes itself completely seriously, and that’s part of its overwhelming appeal. Fights break out between LaRusso’s Miyagi-Do and Johnny’s more aggressive Cobra Kai students, truces are made, and ghosts from their past return in the form of John Kreese (Martin Kove), Ali Mills (Elisabeth Shue), and Terry Silver (Thomas Ian Griffith). Yet Cobra Kai is more than a nostalgia grab with a training-montage-friendly soundtrack. The expansive young cast can more than hold their own, forging friendships and rivalries as they step out of their sensei’s shadows.

There’s no shortage of TV shows that put usually law-abiding people in circumstances that force them to break the law. Yet Ozark takes a different approach: the main characters are morally compromised from the start. Jason Bateman stars as financial advisor Marty Byrde, who has spent a decade laundering money for a cartel, but after his business partner is caught stealing, Marty has to uproot his family to the Ozarks to launder hundreds of millions of dollars.

Laced with dark humor and unexpected turns, Ozark keeps the tension mounting through its four seasons as each problem they solve only creates a fresh crisis that threatens to unravel them. While all the performances are electric, the show’s MVP is Laura Linney as Marty’s wife Wendy. She goes from a bored stay-at-home mom who gave up her career to someone getting dangerously comfortable in the criminal world; Wendy is in her element, making shady deals and power plays. For her, it’s about letting her true self take the wheel after years of being on autopilot. Not all crime dramas are so willing to take their characters to such dark places and still keep their audience completely invested, but Ozark manages that right up until the final credits roll.

Created by Joe Penhall and produced by David Fincher, Mindhunter is the second show on this list to end prematurely. Set in the late 1970s, the series charts the groundbreaking work of the Behavioral Science Unit. FBI agents Holden Ford (Jonathan Groff) and Bill Tench (Holt McCallany) team up with brilliant psychologist Wendy Carr (Anna Torv) to interview some of the most notorious serial killers to understand how they think and build psychological profiles that may help catch other killers.

Much like Fincher’s Se7en and Zodiac, Mindhunter focuses more on the psychology behind the murders and the toll it takes on the team that’s trying to make sense of the madness. The interviews with Edmund Kemper (Cameron Britton) are incredibly tense. He speaks calmly and intelligently about the truly terrible things he has done. The first season is more about gathering information; the second puts their theories into practice by investigating the Atlanta child murders. Now that Fincher won’t be renewing his deal with Netflix, a revival seems unlikely, but the two existing seasons are an engrossing and frequently disturbing crime drama from a master of the genre.

Four Netflix characters, four completely different ways of showing up for somebody. This is the one who fits how you actually do friendship, and what having them in your life would be like.

You do not do friendship at half volume, and neither does he. What you want is somebody who says yes to the plan before you have finished describing the plan.

You are the friend who makes things happen, and so is she. Together you would be mildly exhausting to everyone around you and completely delighted with yourselves.

You have no patience for pleasant lies, and she has no ability to tell them. What looks like coldness from the outside is the most reliable friendship on this list.

You look after whoever is having the worst day. So does he. It is the least strategic way to move through the world and the best possible quality in a friend.

Tina Fey and Robert Carlock’s effervescent sitcom might have a premise that sounds like an emotionally devastating crime drama, but Unbreakable Kimmy Schmidt is one of Netflix’s best original comedies. Kimmy Schmidt (Ellie Kemper) emerges alongside three other women from a bunker they’ve been held in for 15 years under the rule of a charismatic cult leader (Jon Hamm). Once the media frenzy dies down and the shock that the world didn’t end wears off, Kimmy sets out to start a new life in New York City.

Kimmy’s upbeat resilience is tested by everyday life, and her lack of people skills often gets her into trouble, but her newfound friends help her in their own, often odd ways. Deluded out-of-work actor Titus Andromedon (Tituss Burgess), a wild-card landlady (Carol Kane), and her unhinged socialite boss (Jane Krakowski) all become her eclectic support system. As you’d expect from the makers of 30 Rock, the jokes arrive fast and ridiculous, with a supporting cast full of scene stealers. Even though the interactive special Kimmy vs. The Reverend has been removed alongside all of Netflix’s choose-your-own-adventure-style shows, the four seasons remain a joyfully absurd comedy masterpiece.

Proving that adult animation could be more than just obscenities and crass jokes, BoJack Horseman was an early Netflix original and one they’ve yet to surpass. Created by Raphael Bob-Waksberg, the series stars Will Arnett as the titular washed-up actor, with his days on a hit sitcom a thing of the past, along with his sense of purpose and relevance in the ever-changing entertainment industry. He attempts to mount a comeback by writing a tell-all memoir, and the publisher sends ghostwriter Diane (Alison Brie) to get an up-close-and-personal look at the horse behind the star.

Intertwined with Hollywood satire and the obvious comedy mined from a world where anthropomorphic animals and humans live side by side, BoJack Horseman is a far deeper show than its glossy animation suggests. Delving into his traumatic childhood, his adult depression, and problems with addiction, BoJack continuously tries to work on himself, but his self-destructive nature becomes hard for his friends to sit by and watch. The writing never excuses his behavior or shies away from showing the hurtful repercussions of his actions or paints the road to recovery as anything less than hard work. While the series has a constant melancholy, BoJack Horseman is a beautifully woven story that uses a horse to show us what it means to be human.

Baran bo Odar and Jantje Friese’s elegant German sci-fi series frequently appears on lists of Netflix’s best original shows, and with good reason. The small town of Winden has known many tragedies, and many of its residents have spent generations hiding secrets that connect four families in unexpected and unsettling ways. Grappling with his father’s sudden death and the mysterious disappearance of his friend Mikkel, Jonas (Louis Hofmann) embarks on a journey to uncover the past and prevent an apocalyptic future.

Few TV shows have successfully pulled off such ambitious creative swings and told a richly complex yet rewarding story, but Dark makes it look easy when all its puzzle pieces fall into place. Despite the global ramifications, the emotional stakes of its time-travel mystery remain personal, especially in the complicated relationship between Jonas and Mikkel’s sister, Martha (Lisa Vicari). Naturally, for a series that deals with grief, time travel, and alternate universes, there’s a lot to take in, so repeat viewing is almost essential, as even slower episodes prove vital by the time it reaches its heartbreaking conclusion.

While Liz Flahive and Carly Mensch’s 1980s-set series was another TV casualty of the COVID-19 pandemic, with the final season canceled after filming the first episode, GLOW remains perfect from beginning to end. Out-of-work actress Ruth Wilder (Alison Brie) attends an open audition held by B-movie director Sam Sylvia (Marc Maron) to try out for a new television series, The Gorgeous Ladies of Wrestling. Among the diverse mix of women looking for a second chance or their big break, Ruth’s former best friend Debbie (Betty Gilpin) arrives to confront her and ends up joining the team.

Their rivalry eventually spills into the wrestling ring, with Debbie taking on the heroic role of Liberty Belle and Ruth playing Russian villain Zoya. All the cast completely commit to their wrestling personas no matter how outlandish; they each bring something different and make the women behind them feel real. For all the neon lights, spandex, and training montages to iconic '80s music, what sits at the heart of GLOW is friendship. From characters learning to stand on their own to the tentative steps Ruth and Debbie take to rebuild their fractured bond, GLOW is a heartfelt series that prizes characters as much as power slams.

Joint Letter on Low-Carbon Steel Credits in the Review of EU CO₂ Emission Standards for Cars

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Creating predictable demand for genuinely low-carbon steel is urgent. If properly designed, low-carbon steel credits can create a lead market for green steel and secure a future for a green steel industry in Europe.

Many green steel projects have been announced across Europe in recent years. However, some are facing setbacks due to a lack of offtake for low-carbon steel products. Without strong and timely lead market policies, Europe risks losing industrial capacity, jobs and technological leadership.

Creating predictable demand for genuinely low-carbon steel is therefore urgent. If properly designed and unencumbered by additional flexibility or extensions of scope of the car CO₂ emission standards Regulation, low-carbon steel credits can create a lead market for green steel and secure a future for a green steel industry in Europe.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

Jaguars-Ravens averages 13.96 million on Prime Video

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

When it comes to interpreting press releases regarding the audiences for sporting events, there’s a rule of thumb.

The higher the number appears in the release, the better the company thinks the audience is. The lower the number appears in the release, the less impactful the figure is.

And if the number is really bad, there’s no release at all.

For the Week 9 Thursday night game between the Jaguars and Ravens— which was destined to have a non-stellar audience thanks to the Ravens being down to their third-string quarterback — Prime Video led with the fact that the eight-game season-to-date average has outperformed last year’s numbers.

The 33-10 win by the Jaguars, which Prime Video accurately called “lopsided” in the release, generated an average of 13.96 million viewers.

It nevertheless was, as Prime Video notes, a 13-percent bump over last year’s Week 9 Thursday night game. And for good reason. The Week 9 Thursday night game in 2025 featured the Ravens at the Dolphins, a 28-6 Baltimore win.

Even with an audience of less than 14 million in the eighth TNF game of the season, the package is offering 17.15 million per week for 2026.

This week’s game — Commanders at Giants — should do well, given that the Giants are both doing well and occupy the nation’s top market. In Week 11, it’s Colts at Texans. Which won’t be great.

The following week, Prime Video has a potentially massive game on Black Friday between the currently 7-2 Broncos and 6-2 Steelers.

After that, it’s Chiefs-Rams, Vikings-Patriots, 49ers-Chargers, Texans-Eagles (on Christmas Eve), and Ravens-Bengals.

Tesla tuner Unplugged offers to take over Cybertruck production

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Unplugged Performance, the biggest name in Tesla aftermarket tuning, wants to keep the Cybertruck alive. The company publicly offered on Tuesday to take the Cybertruck line off Tesla’s hands, funded by a consortium of investors.

It comes just four days after Elon Musk said Tesla would wind down Cybertruck production at Giga Texas around mid-2027, with the truck selling at roughly 12% of the 250,000 units a year Tesla planned for.

UP posted the pitch on X as “an open offer to Tesla.” The company says it fully supports putting Cybercab first and would run Cybertruck production “at no cost to Tesla’s floor space, headcount, or focus.”

In exchange, Tesla would collect a license fee plus FSD and Robotaxi network revenue on every vehicle built. UP even offered to let Cybercab get first call on battery cells. “We’ll wait our turn,” the company wrote.

UP founder Ben Schaffer made it clear that he supports Tesla’s all-in move to autonomy, but he wants to keep the Cybertruck program alive.

And it isn’t stopping at the pickup. UP says it would build the Cybertruck, a three-row “CyberSUV,” and an unmanned “CyberCargo” hauler. Two vehicles Tesla has sometimes referenced, but they were never unveiled.

The post passed 240,000 views within hours, and the replies are mostly Cybertruck owners and Tesla shareholders begging Musk to say yes. Neither Musk nor Tesla had publicly responded at the time of writing.

UP has a lot riding on this. It sells an entire Cybertruck product line, the UP Invincible lift and armor kits, and it has scored several contracts to turn Cybertrucks into police vehicles for law enforcement agencies. CEO Ben Schaffer once described the company’s purpose as doing “the things that Tesla maybe wants to do, but doesn’t have the bandwidth to do.” This is that idea taken to its extreme.

Musk framed the Cybertruck’s end as a space problem. He said Tesla needs to “make room for increased Cybercab production.”

If the Cybertruck program was successful, Tesla wouldn’t be killing it. Tesla also produces Model Ys at Giga Texas, and it is not shutting that down to make room for Cybercab.

As we reported when Tesla announced the wind-down, that explanation doesn’t hold up. Giga Texas has more than 10 million square feet of floor space, the Cybertruck line runs at about a quarter of its 125,000-unit installed capacity, and Texas had only a few hundred Cybercabs registered as of late October.

The real problem is buyers. Tesla delivered 8,295 “Other Models” in Q3, and we estimated 7,000 to 8,000 of those were Cybertrucks, a pace of about 30,000 trucks a year. That was a good quarter by Cybertruck standards.

Even that number flatters it. S&P Global Mobility data showed SpaceX bought 1,279 Cybertrucks in Q4 2025, about 18% of US registrations that quarter. Tesla also tried a cheaper RWD trim, killed it, launched a $60,000 AWD version, then raised prices by $5,000 in August. None of it fixed demand.

I’ll give UP credit for this: the offer calls Musk’s bluff. If the Cybertruck is really dying because Cybercab needs the room, here’s someone volunteering to take the line off Tesla’s plate. Problem solved.

Of course, it’s more complicated than that.

There are many ways this could fail or never happen in the first place, but there’s also a slight possibility that makes this possible.

First off, the backlash is real. If you look on social media and forums, yes, most of the discussion is about how Cybertruck is a failure, and Elon is lying about why Tesla is killing it, but there’s also a serious group of people, especially Cybertruck owners, who are genuinely upset that Tesla is killing the program because they love their trucks.

Most people have never driven a Cybertruck, but the steer-by-wire makes it a very addictive vehicle to drive, and even though the price is high, the value proposition for the specs is not bad compared to the very limited options for electric pickup trucks.

In other words, I wouldn’t necessarily bet against this effort, especially if they add an SUV variant and show the program some love with some tweaks here and there.

I could see a group of wealthy Cybertruck fans pooling money under Unplugged to make this happen, but getting Tesla to agree could be hard. It’s not unheard of for an automaker to sell a vehicle program to a dealer, like the Lotus Seven and Studebaker Avanti, but it would be a first at this scale.

You could make sense of it by looking at UP to Tesla as AMG to Mercedes. It’s not too big a stretch, considering UP isn’t just your average Tesla tuner. It even worked with the automaker on its own version of the Model S Plaid.

If UP were to become for Tesla what AMG has become for Mercedes, it could make some sense for the company to run a separate vehicle program that started under Tesla, but even as I write this, I realize that there’s very little chance this will happen.

But I wish them the best of luck, and I would be happy to see the Cybertruck live on.

This '60s Japanese Thriller Is One Of The Best Movies Streaming On HBO Max

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Ben Affleck's new kidnapping thriller "Animals" made a splash on Netflix this year — at least in terms of viewership. Critics dismissed the picture, and justifiably so, given how shaky the "Animals" script is. Yours truly clocked that "Animals" is basically an unauthorized remake of Akira Kurosawa's "High and Low," and I was not the only one.

In both movies, a rich man who has just invested all his money in a venture (a business takeover in "High and Low," a mayoral campaign in "Animals") receives a phone call that his son has been kidnapped. Except, the ransomers mistakenly took one of his much poorer employee's sons — so, does he still bankrupt himself to save a kid that isn't his?

I would highly suggest turning off Netflix and flipping over to HBO Max (where "High and Low" is currently streaming) to watch Akira Kurosawa show you how it's done. Several of the filmmaker's classics are currently streaming on there, including "Seven Samurai," "Rashomon," "Throne of Blood," and "Yojimbo." Like with "High and Low," all of those movies also star Toshiro Mifune, Kurosawa's go-to leading man at his peak. But "High and Low" is my personal favorite of the bunch and Kurosawa's magnum opus.

Kurosawa is distinguished for his samurai movies or Jidaigeki (Japanese historical dramas). He often adapted William Shakespeare's plays, too, moving their stories from Europe to to the east: "Throne of Blood" is literally just "Macbeth" in feudal Japan, and it's one of the best "Macbeth" movies to boot.

"High and Low" stands out in that collection for its contemporary setting and source material. (It's adapted from a procedural novel, "King's Ransom," by American author Ed McBain.) While he may not have had the Bard to draw on this time, Kurosawa still made some pure cinematic brilliance.

If I were ever tasked with assembling a list of "old" and/or international movies to convince people that black and white or non-English-language movies can be great, "High and Low" would be on there, 100%. That's not just because it's great (it is), but it's great popular entertainment: a taut suspense thriller and investigative procedural. It's not at all "pretentious," as untrained viewers might call it.

"Someone wants a ransom for the wrong kid, but only you can pay it" is also a great moral dilemma for a thriller and one that slots neatly into anywhere in the world with class disparity. That's why "High and Low" has been remade in many different settings. We know what a hero would do in this situation, but we're not sure if Kingo Gondo (Toshiro Mifune) is a hero from the beginning.

Now, the movie wisely stacks the deck in Gondo's favor so he doesn't come off as an aloof rich guy. He wasn't born rich, he worked his way up, so he knows what losing everything is like, and that makes the sacrifice extra painful. And while he works in shoe manufacturing, he's got a creative spirit. He's planning a leveraged buyout because he wants to make high-quality shoes his way instead of saving money on a cheaper product.

Would you sacrifice all your savings and life's work to save an innocent child? You probably just answered "yes," but it's still an abstract question for you. "High and Low" portrays how someone would act when the choice and its consequences become real. The most impressive balancing act in the movie is that it both does and doesn't feel like a foregone conclusion when he agrees to pay the ransom.

"High and Low" doesn't end when the ransom gets paid. The kidnapper escapes, setting off the second half, where Gondo is largely absent and the police characters take over to try and find the kidnapper. "High and Low" is as bifurcated as its title, with the two halves divided by setting: Gondo's hilltop mansion in the first half, the town below in the second, with the train sequence where Gondo delivers the ransom as a fitting bridge. (It's worth noting that a more literal translation of the original Japanese title, "Tengoku to Jigoku," is "Heaven and Hell.")

During that first half, Kurosawa uses Gondo's living room like a theater stage. Even confined to a single house, the picture looks huge. The screen is wide enough that you can keep track of each character's movements, and the film blocks no frame carelessly. The contained setting, and immense pressure placed on Gondo, still give the first half a claustrophobic feeling. The second half entering the town below is a pressure release, but your attention doesn't go slack because there's still a mystery to solve. The ending of "High and Low," where Gondo and the kidnapper finally come face-to-face, is a triumphant denouement.

Many great American filmmakers have looked to Akira Kurosawa, like "The Godfather" director Francis Ford Coppola. Watching "High and Low," it's easy to understand why.

PetMed Express, Inc. (PETS) Q2 2027 Earnings Call Prepared Remarks Transcript

Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce Get Neighbor’s Support for 8-Foot Privacy Wall at $5.35 Million Ohio Estate

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce’s plan to build an 8-foot privacy wall at their $5.35 million Ohio estate is headed back before a local review board, and at least one neighbor says the proposal has more support than the backlash suggests.

The Kansas City Chiefs tight end, 36, snapped up the eight-bedroom lakefront mansion in March for $5.35 million.

Since then, Kelce and Swift have reportedly taken steps to keep prying eyes away from the historical residence, including introducing plans to construct an 8-foot wall around the property—which were rejected in September.

Speaking anonymously to Page Six ahead of the board’s next meeting, the neighbor pushed back on the idea that the proposal had angered residents.

“I think they’re excited. I think it’s great. I don’t think people are angry,” the neighbor told the outlet.

The Bratenahl Architectural Review Board previously considered the proposal on Aug. 11 and Sept. 8 and is scheduled to take it up again on Nov. 10.

The proposed wall appears to conflict with local regulations, which state that solid fences and walls—as well as fences with less than 75% openness—cannot exceed 30 inches in height.

According to TMZ, a new proposal has since been submitted, although it remains unclear what changes have been made to the original plans.

The setback came after the couple implemented other stringent security measures at the property.

Page Six previously reported that the residence is equipped with a sophisticated security system capable of issuing verbal warnings to anyone who ventures too close.

“A neighbor approached the house and got a voice warning about not passing onto the property after getting too close to the house,” a nearby resident told the outlet.

Built in 1895, the residence spans more than 21,000 square feet and sits on approximately 3.5 acres along Lake Erie.

The mansion boasts eight bedrooms and 11 bathrooms, as well as multiple fireplaces, a home theater, game room, wine cellar, fitness center, sauna, and heated pool overlooking the lake.

The estate is located near Cleveland and is also close to a private airport, according to the outlet—offering the star athlete a convenient launching point for trips with his wife, 36.

Kelce purchased the dwelling from Figurati Construction Group, which is owned by Gary Pellegrino. The company acquired the property for $1.95 million in 2023.

The home was subsequently listed for $6.99 million in 2024 before its price was reduced to $5.99 million in May 2025. Kelce ultimately secured the estate for $5.35 million.

Located in Bratenahl, OH, the property sits just a few miles from Cleveland Heights, where Kelce grew up alongside his older brother, Jason Kelce.

The tiny village is nestled along the southern shore of Lake Erie and offers a mix of multimillion-dollar estates and more modest residences. Homes currently on the market in the area range in price from roughly $250,000 to $2.4 million, according to Realtor.com® data.

The transaction was represented by David Ayers and Nick Zawitz of The Agency Cleveland.

“We’re thrilled for the new owners and so excited for Cleveland,” Ayers and Zawitz said in a statement.

News of their purchase came just one day after it was confirmed that Kelce and Swift added a fluffy white dog to their family.

He introduced the couple's adorable white pup to the world while starring in Tommy Hilfiger's fall 2026 ad campaign.

The sweet cameo confirmed months of speculation that Kelce and Swift had quietly expanded their family with a Samoyed in 2025.

Just days after Kelce and Swift wed, it was revealed that the pup had become such a key part of their lives that it even played a role in their wedding celebrations.

The "Lover" singer is known to have a very soft spot for her own pets: three cats, named Meredith Grey, Olivia Benson, and Benjamin Button.

It's not yet known how the felines have responded to the family's newest member.

However, the dog playing such a key role in Swift and Kelce's wedding ceremony indicates what a big part of their lives it has become—with a framed photo of the pup taking center stage in one of the few glimpses of the venue leaked since the wedding.

A spokesperson for the couple did later confirm reports that the ceremony was officiated by Adam Sandler, while also sharing details about the couple's wedding-day looks.

According to the official release, both the bride and groom sported Christian Dior Haute Couture, Christian Louboutin shoes, and Cartier jewelry—and neither had a bridal party. Instead, they relied on their siblings to be by their side during the ceremony.

“Taylor and Travis did not have bridesmaids or groomsmen. Austin Swift served as Taylor’s Man of Honor, Jason Kelce was Travis’ Best Man," the statement read.

According to People, the singer walked down the aisle to an acoustic version of her hit "Love Story."

The A-list couple joined a long line of celebrities who have wed in New York City, where Swift has owned property for more than a decade.

Kelsi Karruli is a senior celebrity reporter who joined Realtor.com in 2025. She writes about trends encompassing real estate and celebrity properties. She previously wrote for Daily Mail, True Urban Culture, and JMedia Corporation, where she specialized in covering celebrities, relationships, psychology, and gender issues. She has a bachelor's degree in journalism from Pace University, where she received the James Rose Award for excellence in journalism. When she isn’t updating readers on A-listers' moves, she is spending time with her cat, Mary, and cheering on the Albanian soccer team.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: helping a sibling

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

... i figure that $500/mo will roughly cover her medicare and dental insurance premiums for her remaining lifetime.
Ummm. . . Do you know how Medicare works and what options she has (or will have)?

We're in our late 70s and the Part B supplemental plan for each of us is about $500 a month. The Medicare premium is just over $200 and the supplemental plan is about $300 for our age group. Drug plan is extra cost and depends on what meds you take. If you want dental insurance, that is outside of Medicare and we each pay about $50 a month for that. . .

I don't mean to hijack this thread but you may need to reconsider your plan.
Exactly. Assuming no IRMAA, the part B premiums are now well over $200 and the medigap can be quite large as she gets older, as the poster comments above. My plan G at age 73 and a half is about $400 a month and I expect it to go up to maybe 500 next year. Dental insurance is of limited value and a few root canals and implants can be many thousands of dollars.
