Mamdani Begins Reconstruction of Historic Chinatown Building Decimated in 2020 Fire

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Some $184 million will go toward the revival of 70 Mulberry Street, which once housed community-based organizations.

On Monday, November 9, New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani, along with the commissioners for the departments of design and construction, citywide administrative services, and cultural affairs, commenced the $184 million reconstruction project of 70 Mulberry Street, a historically significant building that sustained severe damage from a fire in 2020.

Upon the project's expected completion in 2030, 70 Mulberry Street will house or provide additional space for the five community organizations that operated there before the fire, including the Museum of Chinese in America (MOCA). The new building will include artist Jennifer Wen Ma's "Wings of Everchange 易之翼," a permanent, three-part art installation commissioned by the Department of Cultural Affairs’ (DCLA) Percent for Art program.

Working between Beijing and New York City, Ma has produced varied interdisciplinary media ranging from "cleansing walks," which appear like one-person funerary processions, to collections of plants coated in Chinese ink.

Designed by Grimshaw Architects, the new building retains architectural traces of the 1892 building, while expanding to include six community rooms, multipurpose areas, outdoor spaces, and several modern accommodations. The renovated building is expected to provide approximately 30% more public space than the original facility and will extend three floors higher.

Founded and constructed in 1892 as Public School 23, the building was repurposed into a community hub in the mid-1970s. After the fire devastated much of the building's structure in 2020, the five organizations operating there relocated to nearby venues. While MOCA had moved to its 215 Centre Street flagship in 2009, it retained the old gallery space at 70 Mulberry Street as an archival space and research library for the museum's 85,000-item collection, most of which was salvaged from the fire.

Since accepting a $35 million city capital grant tied to the construction of a Chinatown jail in 2019, MOCA has come under intense scrutiny. In July, protesters condemned museum leaders for accepting the so-called "jail money" in exchange for their complicity in the jail's construction and the possible devastation to Chinatown's communities.

Ma's public installation touches upon much of the Mulberry Street site's historical record and its role in Chinatown in three capacities.

The central sculpture in the building's atrium will be modeled after the graceful brushstrokes of Chinese calligraphy and incorporates salvaged materials from the 2020 fire. Within the sculpture, glass orbs serve as time capsules dedicated to certain individuals or groups touched by 70 Mulberry Street. Ma also plans to launch a publicly accessible website and app with oral testimonies from 70 community members about the building. The third part of the installation extends to the historic spiral stairwell, where ink paintings on glass panels snake up several floors along the inner stair tower.

The New York City Public Design Commission unanimously approved Ma's design in January 2024 as part of the Percent for Art program, which mandates that 1% of eligible funds for city-backed construction projects be spent on public artworks.

"Our community has gone through so many challenges and changes," Ma said in a statement. "I set out to make a work that would embrace these changes, honoring our history, anchoring us in the present and taking us soaring into the future.”

This residency at the U-M Stamps School, in partnership with the U-M Museum of Art (UMMA), offers artists a $20,000 honorarium, housing, studio space, and a culminating exhibition in UMMA’s prominent Stenn Gallery.

The Bayeux tapestry, a new biography of Edward Hopper, The Met's exhibition catalogue on Krasner and Pollock, what architecture is, and more

Funding for tuition, healthcare, and stipends is available. Apply to the University of Maryland, Baltimore County program by Feb 1 to be considered for financial support.

Generously funded, renowned faculty and visiting artists, spacious studios, and an exhibition at the Manetti Shrem Museum of Art.

‘We Need to Bring Tom Cruise Home to Make Movies Here’: U.S. Film Incentive Takes Center Stage at AFM

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Tom Cruise may not have much of a presence at the American Film Market, more accustomed to selling movies with considerably lower budgets than those the star is used to. But on the opening day of the 2026 edition his name was casually dropped as part of a major pitch for the much-discussed federal tax incentive.

The initiative, which many in the industry hope will help turn around a major slump in terms of U.S.-shot productions, took center stage at the AFM on day one, which launched with uncharacteristic levels of optimism, at least for Hollywood (and assuming the bill is approved). Related Stories 'Carrie' Renewed for Season 2 at Amazon Amazon Ad Revenue Growth Slows in Q3, Ecommerce Giant Undershoots Earnings Estimates

“Star salaries is a key issue, because what we’re looking to do is bring back the biggest movies with the biggest amount of jobs attached to them,” said the MPA’s SVP of federal government affairs Josh Rogin. “So when Tom Cruise does a movie in France, all those jobs become French jobs. All the taxes he pays, he pays it to the French government. Our current system is actually supporting runaway productions in this way. We need to bring Tom Cruise home to make movies here!”

Rogin was speaking on the first panel of the AFM alongside Stephen Paul, chair and CEO of SP Media Group, and SP Media Group president Scott Karol, who both met with President Donald Trump at the White House earlier this year — alongside “special ambassador” to Hollywood Jon Voight — to discuss the proposed incentive.

While the exact details of the Oval Office meeting were kept under wraps, Paul did discuss the letter read to Trump by Voight that they had all worked on.

“It explained everything about the industry, explaining about how we’ve not only lost movies from around the world, but we’re losing the effect of America on the rest of the world,” he said. “Because when we don’t make films here in America, people aren’t seeing that around the world. We’re faking it. We’re saying, ‘Oh, this is America,’ but filming it in England or filming in New Zealand. People all around the world have wanted to come to America because of the films that they have seen and television that have filmed in America.”

As has been previously reported, Paul told Trump about a film he was developing about another U.S. president, RIchard Nixon. “I said, Mr. President, I’m ashamed about what I’m about to tell you, but I’m about to make a film about a president of the United States that I’m going to film in England because I can’t afford to film it here.”

According to Paul, when he asked Trump if he thought that “was right,” he said “No,” prompting Karol to show him all the information about how the federal tax credit would help.

The efforts seemingly worked, with Trump later posting on Truth Social that “Congress should approve, immediately, a Federal Production Incentive to create Entertainment Jobs in America.”

But there’s now renewed urgency to get the bill passed — with the speakers pointing towards an end-of-year deadline. In terms of the logistics of film development, Karol said it was important for producers so they could give a Jan. 1 2027 start-of-production date — enabling their films to qualify. But he added that there were fresh concerns following the U.S. midterms.

“Because who the hell knows what’s going to happen next year when Democrats come in and we hit the House and Senate and Trump’s upset because he’s a lame duck president — we don’t want that,” he said.

In terms of the impact of the incentive, Rogin laid it out starkly in terms that AFM’s array of international visitors would understand.

“A federal incentive would change the entire nature of our domestic film and TV industry. Right now, we are about 39% of the market we produce in America for this audience. We used to be at 60% just a few years ago,” he said. “Currently, America is in last place in federal incentives. We’re dead last. We have none. So there are dozens and dozens of countries that have created competitive tax requirements that draw our production overseas. We don’t need to take the best incentive, we just need to be competitive.”

PlayStation Cancels In-Development Live-Service Shooter, Fairgames, At Jade Raymond's Former Haven Studio

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

PlayStation has reportedly canceled Fairgames, the long-in-development live-service shooter being made by Haven Studios, which Jade Raymond founded in 2021. Sony acquired Haven in 2022, and in 2025, Raymond left the studio, though it remained dedicated to developing Fairgames without her. It seems the story of Fairgames is now over, according to a new Bloomberg report.

The publication reports that PlayStation will not enforce any job cuts or layoffs as a result of Fairgames' cancellation, which comes as a surprise, and the studio will instead shift to support other first-party teams under the PlayStation umbrella. According to Bloomberg, this cancellation comes after a recent test of the game that allowed "thousands of players" to check it out did not deliver the results PlayStation wanted.

"While the test demonstrated strong operational excellence, live ops readiness and community engagement, player feedback and retention results were mixed and fell short of the goals we had set," PlayStation executives Sean Decker and John Doyle wrote in an email to employees today, according to Bloomberg. "Combined with challenging market conditions in the PVP shooter genre, we have made the difficult decision to cease development on the game."

This doesn't necessarily come as a surprise. PlayStation announced Fairgames back in 2023 and hasn't shown much since, leaving many to assume development has been struggling behind the scenes. Since then, PlayStation has pulled back heavily on its live-service plans, which originally included launching more than 10 live-service games by March of this year. Naughty Dog's Last of Us multiplayer game was canceled, Bungie ended active development on Destiny 2, and live-service Horizon spin-off Hunters Gathering is reportedly being overhauled following poor playtest feedback, amongst other stories. Reading between the lines, many fans were waiting for news like today's cancellation of Fairgames to drop.

Still, in an era where games are canceled and entire teams are laid off (sometimes even after releasing a successful game), it's nice to see Haven employees are not facing layoffs.

Why Ari Aster and Lars Knudsen’s Square Peg Built a Therapy Circle for Hollywood’s Next Generation in Texas

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Talent labs and mentorship programs are plentiful for aspiring filmmakers — but where do you turn when your career has already started, after your first movie or on the precipice of one, and especially when filmmaking can be such a lonely enterprise?

Enter Square Peg Social, the Austin, Texas-based filmmaking summit launched by Lars and Amy Knudsen and Ari Aster that offers four days of singular community-building: brutally honest advice, commiseration, and idea exchange shared among 35 participants, with the program now in its second year.

A weekend of panels, discussion, and camaraderie designed for filmmakers at the early stages of their career, this year’s talent-incubating event was even crashed by the likes of Zach Cregger and Richard Linklater — both of whom were down to get frank about their filmmaking highs and lows. (In fall 2025, IndieWire did a deep dive into Square Peg Social’s first year, just to show how much the event has already grown.) Related Stories ‘Ink’ Star Jack O’Connell on His ‘New Era’ as a Leading Man: ‘Thank F**k for Danny Boyle’ How Casting the New ‘Harry Potter’ Helped ‘Club Kid’ Find Its Adorable 10-Year-Old Star

But the program’s stable of 2026 mentors and panelists — i.e., filmmakers keen not only to open up to new filmmakers about their own struggles, but also to scout new talent — also counted “Primetime” and “You Can See Everything” director Lance Oppenheim, “Sing Sing” filmmaker Greg Kwedar, “The First Omen” director Arkasha Stevenson, and “Mother Mary” director David Lowery among them.

The closed-door, off-the-record, noncompetitive summit opened to applicants in May, later unfolding from September 17-21. Partners including the Austin Film Society, Belvedere, and Airbnb offset the cost, besides a modest application fee, for participants to hole up for four days and nights at Soho House that don’t promise job opportunities — but instead, the kind of lasting industry connections that can take years (and many starts and stops) to build. Also, no job opportunities isn’t entirely true. Take last year’s Square Peg Social mentee Yona Strauss, who’s now working for Knudsen and Aster’s Square Peg.

Just a sampling of this year’s participating producers and directors: producer Annie Huang, who worked on franchise IP like “Barbie” and “Wicked” at major studios; Jack Auen, co-founder of the filmmaking collective Cosmic Salon, which this year directed the New York critics’ favorite “Chronovisor”; Emmy-winning director of the Netflix short “The Turnaround,” Kyle Thrash; SXSW 2026 Neon Auteur Award winner Oliver Woolf; and many more who have their foot in the door, but are ready to cross the threshold.

IndieWire spoke to Lars Knudsen and Aster (who also co-founded the production company Square Peg, which this year backed “The Drama” and “Primetime”), plus Stevenson, Oppenheim, and Kwedar, along with three participants, to replay the on-the-ground experience in Austin. Late nights, early mornings, and vulnerabilities abounded across a long (but in some ways too short) weekend of intimate panels, small group sessions, and candid remarks from established filmmakers about the times they were trying to hold their own projects together with Scotch tape and paper clips. Think of it like a mini summer camp devoted to obsessive ambition.

“When you’ve been doing it for this long, sometimes it feels like you’re just a little bit going through the motions. You’re going from movie to movie to movie, and you sometimes forget why we do this thing that we do. We’re in this world working with these artists where they don’t have a plan B. Having now worked with 30, 40 filmmakers, I realized that when my wife brought up the idea of doing something because she has her own aversions to the industry, and that there isn’t really — it sounds cliché — but there just isn’t a lot of kindness or compassion or openness in our industry.

“There’s a lot of fear, there’s a lot of rejection. We wanted to create a space for filmmakers where there’s radical transparency and where it was truly, truly focused on a selfless thing: helping young filmmakers navigate the industry at a time in their career where things can either take off or not, and really demystifying the process of making movies, especially during the stage where they’re often in the back seat, which is financing or casting. Because for all these filmmakers that I work with and all the filmmakers that we’ve invited there, even the producers, it’s the first thing to think about when they wake up and the last thing to think about when they go to bed. Nothing is more important. I wanted to create an event where that is treated with the utmost importance, almost like it’s a life-or-death thing because it is all they want to do.”

“When Lars and Amy first broached the idea with me, I realized that it was something that I wish I had when I got out of school [at AFI], because I really did have to navigate that on my own. I was making a lot of shorts with whatever money I could raise and writing screenplays and trying to get one screenplay going. When that failed, writing something else and really looking for any sort of mentorship or guidance, It’s a very demoralizing process, and it was very, very hard for me to find traction, and it hasn’t gotten easier. It’s as bad as it’s ever been. There’s just so much uncertainty right now in the industry, not just for filmmakers. Even the lives of these studios are suddenly in danger and in question.

“There are a lot of filmmakers that Lars and I have been championing and films that we’ve been trying to get financing for that have proven almost impossible… It felt like a good thing to create a space where filmmakers can come together and commiserate and share experiences and connect. More important than even the connections that are made between the mentors and the mentees here are actually the relationships made among the people who are attending. A lot of them end up working together because we have producers and directors coming in. Last year proved really successful, and I would just say it was refreshing for us, too, that it galvanized us to keep doing this. Because last year it was almost an experiment: Will this work? How will we feel having done this? … Everybody walked away feeling good and feeling like it was a really useful and positive experience.”

“I’ve never really had the opportunity to just hang out with so many great filmmakers, not only just working filmmakers whose work I’ve loved, like David Lowery, who I’ve idolized for so long, but there’s so many filmmakers that were actually in the program as participants whose work I also have really admired. So having a space where we could all just kind of talk and vent and really nerd out on stuff that you never really get the opportunity to do.

“Because this was so filmmaker-driven and filmmaker-led, it encouraged the spirit of vulnerability [where] everyone really went there. I felt like maybe I should go back to therapy or something, but I felt like this was a great opportunity to therapize and also dump all of my feelings into a forum where you could just be open and honest about the process of making films… Most people had made features before, and were in the process of making features. It felt much more like we were at a film festival and everyone was able to connect with each other in a much deeper way, more than anything I’ve been to before.

“The first night I was there, I stayed up till like three in the morning, and I went on one of those electric scooters, the limes with Lars and Christian [Vasquez], my producer, and a producer from the event, too. We went to Whataburger and just kept talking. I think that was sort of the vibe. I don’t know. I guess everyone was encouraged to hang out for as long as they wanted to and to kick back and relax. There was another night we closed down the Soho House lounge bar, and Amy was like, ‘Let’s keep hanging out.’ So we were outside the bar, and everybody pulled together all of the drinks in their mini bar, and we created an ad hoc bar outside of the Soho House, and we kept drinking and hanging.”

“I don’t think you can really do film if you have a plan B because there’s always an escape hatch, and film requires every single atom of your body, mind and soul. It naturally becomes a very therapeutic place. I think because we’re all in this constant state of obsession, that can get really tiring, and it can get really lonely. … [The industry] attracts very sensitive souls. [With Square Peg Social] I didn’t even realize how badly I needed it until the first year. I’d known Amy and Lars before that, and Amy said that she wanted to start a Social. … I thought it would be much smaller, smaller. It ended up being this great big thing where, yeah, it does feel like a therapy circle, really.

“Everyone was really struck by the safety of the event. There was this unspoken agreement that everybody would be checking your egos at the door, and that this would be a very safe place to share your personal vulnerabilities, which is exceptionally rare in film because resources are so limited, and it’s just naturally a very competitive industry. Letting yourself be very, very vulnerable in front of your peers is something you were, in a sense, trained not to do but sometimes desperately need. At Square Peg, also, the line between mentee and mentor blurs quite a bit, which is why I think it’s so beneficial for everybody who goes there.

“It kind of reminds me of summer camp a little bit because you’re up until two or three in the morning talking to people, and then you’re up at eight again.”

“I always think of this term, like, whenever you take an elevator up, if you get to the top floor, send it back down for someone else to get on. [Square Peg] is one of the busiest production companies working today. To dedicate this weekend for emerging artists to have a forum where they can come and feel completely comfortable being very vulnerable with their ambitions, but also the things that they’re afraid of, and to hear that echoed from industry mentors who are being just as vulnerable about their ambitions and the things that they struggle with and the heartbreaks they’ve had, it’s an amazing table that’s set.

“I had ulterior motives in the, in the sense that Clint [Bentley] and I have started this new company, Ethos, and we have a deal at Netflix to finance films from emerging artists, and so I was also there … to look for talent. We’ll be following up with a lot of these artists to just hear more about the films they want to make. One thing that I appreciated about it was that it wasn’t like a pitch fest. You’re not there to pitch your project. You’re there to soak it in and learn.

“It had this healthy blend of these panels, and not the normal kind of panel. One was a panel of filmmakers led off by Ari and Lars, telling a very, very brutally honest story of how hard one of their films was to make. That kind of created a pathway for how the rest of the weekend would unfold.”

“I think the whole reason anyone works in film is that we were once kids making videos in the woods with our friends, making with a shitty camera. And there’s nothing else in this world, and I think I probably speak for everyone, where I can’t imagine film is the only thing that’s our main passion and hobby and something we can also make a career out of. So there was never a plan B at all. Whenever someone asked me, ‘What do you think you’d do if you couldn’t do anything film-adjacent?’ I have literally no idea, maybe a teacher or something. That’s super nice that Lars and the rest of Square Peg was saying that there is no plan B. There’s only: If it takes longer to get to Plan A, then it takes longer to get to Plan A.

“I remember on the very first day before we even properly began, Lars did a little speech with Ari, and Lars was like, ‘We’re going to be 100 percent transparent with you guys.’ And I remember thinking, ‘Oh, that’s cool, but I wonder how transparent.’ And by the end of it, I was like, ‘Fuck me, that was transparent.’ There really was nothing off the table.”

“It was definitely something that I’ve never experienced before in this industry, to experience that level of candor and to hear these traumatic experiences that they have gone through in not a sensationalistic way, but almost in a very personal and almost relatable way, despite me having never experienced that level of millions of dollars of movie budget making — but on a personal level of how the heartbreaks had felt for them individually and the hardships of filmmaking. Coming into this program, there are so many people within our cohort who are incredibly talented, who have accomplished so many things. And coming into it, oftentimes you’ll have a fear of: ‘OK, how do I compare in relation to their accomplishments?’ But in hearing Lars and Ari talk about their own hardships, I think that’s what influenced me to really share some of mine coming up in the industry and producing independently.

“Some of the traumatic experiences that I have also faced are ones that I feel like I have to not talk about or hide from people, for fear of them looking like mistakes or of me looking like I’m not good enough to be doing this. And we were able to share that, like Ollie [Woolf] and I, in our own little producer’s therapy session with the mentors, with Lars. That was really healing for me.”

“[Ari, Lars, and the mentors are] on, not a different level, but they’re all going through similar issues and this and that, whether it’s a low budget or a higher budget type of ordeal. Everyone’s going through the same thing in their own way, in a sense. Just to know the people that I’ve been looking up to for a very long time, the reason in large part why I got into making films, to know that they’re going through similar things and just keep going, and it’s fine. No matter what level we’re going to be at, there’s still something to navigate. So I thought it was very cool to see, over the course of five days, to hear these individual experiences and stuff and how you could relate it back to what we’re going through right now and where we are.

“It was information overload in the best way possible. At a certain point, I hope I’m retaining everything that was said over those five days because it was nonstop, all useful information. I wasn’t even taking notes at a certain point because I’m like, ‘I can’t write all this stuff down. I’m just going to try and soak it all in.'”

The Trendiest Fragrance From the Year You Graduated School

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

From ’80s spicy florals to today’s gourmand favorites, these are the top scents that defined decades

It almost goes without saying that what’s buzzy in beauty often sends ripples through culture, inspiring a flood of viral social media videos and a rise in consumer spending. While there will always be discussion around the most popular skincare treatments, makeup ideas, and hair looks to date, scent's elusiveness makes it hard to pin down—so we’re taking a closer look at some of the trendiest fragrances from the mid-’80s through the present day.

Our curation of celebrated scents, like Calvin Klein’s CK One eau de toilette, Le Labo’s Santal 33 eau de parfum, and more, is not only a walk down memory lane, but also an accounting of the accords that have lived rent-free in our minds for decades. Though technically invisible, these enduring, dynamic fragrances deserve a spot on any beauty holy grail list. Keep reading for the trendiest perfumes, eaux de toilette, and body mists Bazaar editors have loved over the decades.

Dior’s ’80s-born Poison eau de toilette combines honey and musk accords for a sexy, seductive scent. The warm fragrance has long-lasting sillage and a spicy edge, making it perfect for date night.

Key notes: plum, wild berries, coriander, anise, Brazilian rosewood, tuberose, white honey, cinnamon, incense, carnation, jasmine, rose, orange blossom, vanilla, amber, sandalwood, musk, heliotrope, vetiver, cedarwood

This fresh, floral fragrance from Calvin Klein aims to bottle romance, thanks to a bouquet of notes like freesia, white lily, violet, and rose. Eternity’s iconic campaign was fronted by supermodel Christy Turlington, and the fragrance name itself was inspired by a diamond wedding band once owned by the Duke of Windsor, inscribed with the word “Eternity.”

Key notes: freesia, mandarin, green notes, sage, white lily, marigold, muguet, narcissus, carnation, violet, rose, jasmine, sandalwood, patchouli, amber, musk, heliotrope

If you ever visited Elizabeth Arden’s Red Door spa when the brand was in business, you probably recognize the distinctive color and design of this beloved perfume. Inspired by the ethos of beloved locations nationwide, this popular scent captures the destination spa's grandeur and elegance in its heyday through a bold, floral lens.

Key notes: freesia, red roses, orchids, honey, sandalwood

This star-shaped bottle is almost as iconic as the refillable scent inside. An addictive combination of fruit-forward and gourmand notes, Thierry Mugler’s renowned fragrance is warm and spicy with a unique celestial appeal.Key notes: bergamot, red fruits, patchouli

Elizabeth Taylor was a trendsetter in more ways than one, including the art of fragrance-making. White Diamonds is one of the most noteworthy celebrity brands of all time, and was inspired by the actress’s love of—what else—diamonds, along with flowers and lush gardens.Key notes: Amazon lily, neroli, bergamot, orange, Egyptian tuberose, jasmine, ylang-ylang, Turkish rose, carnation, oak moss, sandalwood, amber, musk, patchouli

Firmenich senior perfumer Clement Gavarry smelled CK One as a teenager and instantly fell in love. “This fragrance was revolutionary and the first gender-neutral fragrance with a construction that, for me, has everlasting freshness, a beautiful trail, and is memorable,” he says. “It captures all of the aspects that make a scent a classic—to this day, it is a modern fragrance that stands in its own category. Truly timeless.”

Tommy Hilfiger was one of the most beloved fashion brands of the ’90s, so it’s no surprise its buzzy Tommy Girl scent is considered one of the most popular as well. The clean, fresh fragrance lends itself to spring and summer months, since its combination of citrus, mint, and green-floral notes is like a breath of fresh air.

Key notes: bergamot, spearmint, jasmine, rose, iced tea accord, sandalwood, amber accord, musk

Clinique’s Happy eau de toilette was a certified hit in the ’90s thanks to its fruity-floral blend that evokes positivity and happiness. The brand even commissioned a third-party neurosensory study to measure fragrance wearers’ conscious and subconscious emotional responses, which found that 97 percent of people feel happy when they smell Happy.

Key notes: ruby red grapefruit, mandarin, bergamot, Hawaiian wedding flower, morning dew orchid, mimosa, transparent wood accord

You might be inspired to dream up a fairy-tale date night or give yourself a little extra self-love after spritzing on this themed fragrance from Ralph Lauren. It opens with top notes of pink pepper and mandarin orange before transitioning to a woodier finish, courtesy of musk and patchouli.

Actress Charlize Theron was one of the muses behind this generational Dior scent for 20 years before Rihanna recently took the reins in 2024. But its celebrity appeal is only one of the reasons J’adore deserves a spot on our list. The enduring warm, fruity-floral perfume was a bestseller shortly after its 1999 release and continues to be a must-have for beauty lovers and Bazaar editors alike.

Key notes: ylang-ylang, Damascus rose, jasmine sambac, jasmine grandiflorum, pear, candied plum, violet, lily-of-the-valley

If you’ve ever owned or been a superfan of Victoria’s Secret’s Love Spell, you’re likely a millennial with superior taste in fragrance mists. Originally launched as part of the brand’s Garden collection, its fruity-floral blend is playful and light with plenty of staying power.

Key notes: red apple, cherry blossom, peach, lily-of-the-valley, jasmine, lilac, airy musk, blonde woods, tamarind

Originally launched in 2001 as an eau de toilette, Dolce&Gabbana’s Light Blue makes it easy to mentally transport yourself abroad to the Mediterranean. The fresh, citrusy scent is easy to layer and beautifully bottles the aquatic essence of the Amalfi Coast.

Key notes: Sicilian lemon, apple, cedarwood

Several adored iterations of Chanel’s Chance eau de parfum exist in 2026, but the original 2002 version set the stage for its now-legendary status in the fragrance world. Beyond its singular rounded bottle, which sets it apart from other prestige scents, Chance’s blend of pink pepper, jasmine absolute, and amber patchouli makes it unforgettable.

Key notes: pink pepper, jasmine absolute, amber patchouli, white musk, vanilla

I personally was borderline obsessed with my bottle of Glow by J.Lo eau de toilette when it first launched in 2002. Not only is its Y2K-coded bottle glamorous, with layered nameplate necklaces, but the floral, musky scent strikes the perfect balance between soapy and powdery without being cloying.

Key notes: orange flower, grapefruit, citrus, rose, sandalwood, amber, jasmine, vanilla, musk, orris

This Y2K fragrance truly smells as delicious as its name implies, with an apple top note leading the perfume as a nod to its origins in New York City. The fresh, floral, and fruity scent doesn’t take itself too seriously, which is part of its playful, whimsical appeal.

Viktor&Rolf’s Flowerbomb eau de parfum isn’t just for floral fragrance aficionados. The trendy scent, first launched in 2005, is grounded by woody notes of patchouli, amber, and musk that round out its feminine accords.

Key notes: peony accord, centifolia, rose, freesia accord

Break out your matching Juicy Couture tracksuit! This popular fragrance from Juicy Couture is made to make your mouth water, combining notes of watermelon, passion fruit, and crème brûlée for a fruity-gourmand scent housed in a bottle that doesn’t shy away from early 2000s glam.

Key notes: watermelon, passion fruit, crème brûlée notes

Bright Crystal eau de parfum is one of Versace’s early fragrance gems, with a fresh floral composition that stands the test of time. The perfume is subtle enough for daily wear, but also impactful enough to make a serious scent statement.

Key notes: pomegranate, yuzu iced accord, vegetal amber, acajou, musk

Another buzzy fragrance with a bottle design as iconic as the scent itself, Marc Jacobs’s Daisy is inspired by innocence and purity. Notes of wild strawberry and violet petals, along with white accords of birch and cedarwood, give the fragrance its unique floral-woody musk. Daisy won the Fragrance Foundation’s FiFi Awards for Women’s Luxe Fragrance of the Year and Women’s Prestige Best Packaging the year after its launch, in 2008.

Key notes: wild strawberry, velvety violet petals, gardenia, jasmine, birch cedarwood

All of fashion’s It girls were reaching for Byredo’s Gypsy Water eau de parfum in 2008, and for good reason. The free-spirited scent is as woody as it is adventurous, with a subtle, balanced—and almost skinlike—finish, an ode to the nomadic customs of Romani culture.

Key notes: bergamot, lemon, juniper berries, pepper, incense, soft iris, pine needle, amber, sandalwood, vanilla

Mississippi Senate: Scott Colom extends tight lead over Hyde-Smith as ballot deadlines hit

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Democratic nominee Scott Colom added slightly to his narrow lead over incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith in the Mississippi Senate election as two key ballot deadlines hit Tuesday.

Colom, a local district attorney, as of 4:30 p.m. ET, was ahead of Hyde-Smith by 2,607 votes, out of 953,547 votes counted, with an estimated 97% of the votes in, according to the Associated Press. That's a margin of just 0.27 percentage points.

Independent candidate Ty Pinkins had 20,034 votes.

Tuesday is the deadline for resolving affidavit or provisional ballots, which are cast by voters whose eligibility is disputed or unclear on Election Day. It is also the deadline for the receipt of mail-in absentee ballots that are postmarked by Election Day, Nov. 3.

Mississippi is one of just two states whose Senate election has not been called. Alaska is the other.

Democrats so far have flipped seven Senate seats held by Republicans going into the election. That gives Democrats a new majority in the Senate of at least 54 seats in the 100-member chamber come January.

Alaska before Election Day was viewed as possibly key to the question of whether Republicans would retain their narrow majority in the Senate.

Mississippi, which has not elected a Democratic senator since the segregationist John Stennis in 1982, was on few political analysts' radar as a possible pickup for Democrats. If Colom wins his race, he would be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the post-Civil War Reconstruction Era.

In Alaska, Democratic nominee Mary Peltola is ahead by just 2,517 votes over incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan, with 218,641 total votes counted, a margin of 1.1 percentage points.

Peltola has 48.6% of the total vote. If neither she nor Sullivan gets more than 50% of the total vote, the race will go to a ranked-choice round, in which the two other candidates, Gerald Heikes and another candidate named Dan Sullivan, will be eliminated, and the second choices of voters who made them their first choice will be allocated between Peltola and the incumbent.

Bella Hadid Purchases $5.5 Million ‘Enchanting Creekside Compound’ in Colorado

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Supermodel Bella Hadid is Colorado-bound.

The Victoria’s Secret angel snapped up a gorgeous creekside compound in Crested Butte, CO, according to the Wall Street Journal.

Hadid, 30, purchased the three-bedroom, three-bathroom retreat for $5.5 million—a sharp decrease from its $6,995,000 asking price, set in June this year.

Listing agent Channing Boucher of LIV Sotheby’s International Realty told the publication that the daughter of the former “Real Housewives of Beverly Hills” star has already resided elsewhere in the Crested Butte area for “several months.”

In fact, she recently gushed about the state in a Nov. 9 Instagram post, shared after attending a Colorado Avalanche hockey game. Hadid posted that she's a "Colorado girl at heart" and that it "was only a matter of time before" she would find her way to the team.

It seems as if Hadid has found a perfect slice of the Centennial State to call her own.

“Tucked away on a private alley in the most desired west side of town, this enchanting creekside compound is a secret sanctuary, impossible to replicate in character or setting,” the listing boasted.

The home is nestled on a 7,841-square-foot lot with 200 feet of Coal Creek frontage. While the dwelling itself was constructed in 2001, it’s a historical property that incorporates two “original 100-year-old coal sheds,” which have been lovingly restored to serve as livable spaces.

Inside, an open floor plan features “soaring 30-foot ceilings,” and design details salvaged from century-old structures include the oversized ceilings beams sourced from a historic Columbia River warehouse, along with iron bolts and other charming items.

“The line between old and new vanish in the craftsmanship,” reads the listing, which also touts the weathered lumber used to construct the exterior and the 18th-century Virgina barn hardwood floors.

It’s easy to see why Hadid would choose the idyllic property as an escape. The listing explains that the home is in a secluded spot at the end of a dead-end gravel lane with only one other home, “free from traffic, tourists, and noise.”

The primary suite is in its own wing and constructed from a former coal shed. Skylights provide stunning views of the natural landscape, while the luxurious bathroom makes for a comfortable sanctuary.

The outdoor space is a striking complement to the intentionally designed home. It includes a creekside patio for dining and entertaining, and a soothing hot tub is located just at the edge of the water.

Though the home seems as if it exists in a world of its own, it’s not far from the vibrant attractions Crested Butte has to offer, including upscale dining and boutique shopping.

Hadid's family has been known to seek out residences that allow them to connect with nature. Mom Yolanda Hadid has a stunning Pennsylvania farm, which she previously told Realtor.com® is a place where she can “heal and reconnect with Mother Earth.”

"Eight years ago, after my divorce and years of battling chronic neurological Lyme disease, I needed to get away from the noise of city life," she explained. "I longed for a sanctuary where I could heal and reconnect with Mother Earth just the way I grew up in Holland.

"When I found the farm, I instantly fell in love. … There were no gates at that time, so I parked on the main road and walked down the long driveway; the energy was unbelievable. I instantly knew I was home even before I saw the buildings."

Yolanda listed the property for $10.88 million at the end of 2025, but pulled it off the market just three months later. However, it is understood that the delisting is temporary, and the property will most likely return to the market in the future.

Urologists Have Some Hot Takes on Whether Men Should Sit or Stand to Pee

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

AROUND THE WORLD—and on Reddit—there’s an eternal debate raging about whether men should sit or stand to pee. In the US, the answer seems like a no-brainer: standing. One YouGov poll found that only 10 percent of American men regularly sit down to pee, while 31 percent said they never do this. (There’s even that viral video years ago of a pastor arguing “a man is someone who pisses against the wall.”)

But that same YouGov poll found that where you wee varies. In Germany, 40 percent of guys actually sit down every time to urinate. About 22 percent of Swedish men, for example, sit down every time, but 28 percent sit down most of the time. Entire threads online are devoted to this question, including a hybrid tactic as a compromise in which a guy pees sitting down at home but stands up at a urinal in public.

Looking beyond preference, is there a right answer to take a leak? One study published in the journal Neurourology and Urodynamics tried to give a definitive answer. Depending on your camp, the answer might validate what you know—or piss you off.

Researchers had no issue finding 52 guys to sign up for this peeing experiment. These dudes had no prior health issues and were between the ages of 18 and 39. The guys were randomly divided into two groups that sat to urinate. Then they would stand to pee. The other group did the same but in reverse order. Between each change in position was a "washout period" of 24 hours where guys did their business as usual.

Researchers tracked how much urine came out and how quickly. So who reigned supreme on the porcelain throne? Well, neither.

Sitting or standing to urinate didn’t change how much pee they got out in each session. There was a slight difference in how fast they got to max flow, though. Men were able to reach their peak speed slightly sooner when they peed sitting down, but researchers say this finding was “unlikely to be clinically meaningful and was partly dependent on a single outlier.” Meaning, one guy was pissing really hard while sitting, while everyone else peed at a similar rate and volume no matter what position they did it.

The findings are not surprising to S. Adam Ramin, MD, urologist and medical director of Urology Cancer Specialists. “If you're a normal, young, healthy male, it will make no difference whether you sit or stand to urinate,” he says. “You will have a good stream either way.”

THE FINDINGS WOULD likely be different for older men, especially those with an enlarged prostate. “Sometimes in older men with an obstructing prostate, sitting to urinate may help them to empty their bladders more effectively as they are able to generate more abdominal force to squeeze the urine out,” says Boback Berookhim, MD, urologist at Northwell’s Lenox Hill Hospital.

HEALTHWISE, NOT REALLY. But if you have a certain preference for one over the other, then there’s no harm in sticking with one position. There's some convenience with standing to pee: You pull it out, do your business, and move on. Peeing this way is “likely to save time,” says Dr. Berookhim.

Sitters, on the other hand, might prefer the position because it’s slightly more hygienic. Dr. Ramin says guys are more likely to splash, sprinkle on the toilet seat, or even miss entirely when they’re standing versus sitting. Plus, sitters might be more likely to wash their hands after every use. Men are 1.5 times less likely to wash their hands than women after peeing—probably where they could use those saved seconds.

Beyond that necessary bathroom etiquette, there’s really no other reason to switch up your tried-and-true urinary habits. Dr. Berookhim says he certainly wouldn’t tell a young, healthy guy to change the way they are using the bathroom if they are already doing fine.

Korin Miller is a seasoned freelance writer specializing in general wellness, sexual health and relationships, and lifestyle trends, with work appearing in Men’s Health, Women’s Health, Everyday Health, Food & Wine, and more. She has a master’s degree from American University and lives by the beach.

How the Congressional Review Act lets Congress overturn agency rules

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The Congressional Review Act lets Congress cancel federal agency rules, and these 10 explainers cover how it works, from major rules and Senate fast-track votes to the lookback period

Congress writes the laws, but federal agencies write many of the detailed rules that carry the force of law. Lawmakers routinely hand agencies the authority to fill in details on workplace safety, financial markets, energy use and much more. That delegation raises a basic question about accountability. Once an agency finalizes a rule, how can Congress take back a specific decision it never voted on?

The Congressional Review Act is Congress's statutory answer. Passed in 1996 and signed by President Bill Clinton, the law gives Congress a set procedure to overturn individual rules issued by executive-branch agencies. It does not require lawmakers to rewrite the underlying statute. Congress instead passes a short measure called a joint resolution of disapproval, aimed at a single rule.

Both chambers must pass the same resolution, which then goes to the president. That final step shapes much of how the law is used. The president can veto the resolution, and overriding a veto takes two-thirds of each chamber. Successful uses have mostly come when the president and congressional majorities share a party and the targeted rule came from a predecessor.

Timing also shapes the outcome. The law sets windows for introducing resolutions and for using a special Senate procedure that sidesteps the filibuster. Through the lookback period, rules submitted late in a congressional session can become targets for the next Congress. That leaves rules finalized late in an administration exposed when power changes hands.

A successful disapproval has lasting effects. The canceled rule is treated as though it never took effect. The agency also faces limits on issuing a similar rule later, under statutory language that has never been precisely defined.

The law produced no successful disapproval until 2001. That first success stood alone for years. That changed in 2017, when a Republican Congress and President Donald Trump used it to overturn many Obama-era rules.

The 10 slides that follow explain how Congress cancels federal rules under this law. They cover what counts as a rule, how agencies report to Congress, the special treatment of major rules and the procedures in each chamber. They also explain the president's role and what happens after a disapproval. Each slide stands on its own, so readers can start with whichever part of the process they need.

Congress passed the Congressional Review Act in 1996, and President Bill Clinton signed it into law on March 29 of that year. The law gives Congress a formal way to overturn rules that executive-branch agencies issue under powers Congress handed to them.

The act did not arrive as a standalone bill. Lawmakers enacted it as one part of a larger package, the Contract with America Advancement Act. Clinton's signature on that broader measure put the review process into federal law.

The statute is codified at 5 U.S.C. sections 801 to 808. Those eight sections hold the full framework, from how agencies report new rules to what happens after Congress acts on them. That placement gives the process a fixed legal address. Lawmakers and agencies work from the same set of provisions each time a rule comes up for review.

The law responds to a problem that starts with delegation. Congress often writes statutes in broad terms. It then assigns the detailed work of carrying them out to federal agencies.

Those agencies write rules that fill in the specifics. Congress does not vote on each of those rules before an agency issues it. The authority to write them comes from statutes Congress has already passed.

The 1996 law added a check rather than a rewrite of that system. Agencies kept the rulemaking authority Congress had already granted them. What changed was that Congress gained a set procedure for answering a finished rule.

The law's scope follows from that arrangement. It applies to rules from executive-branch agencies acting under authority Congress delegated. The branch that granted the power keeps a statutory path to cancel a particular use of it.

The Congressional Review Act covers a wide range of agency actions. Its definition of "rule" comes from the Administrative Procedure Act, the law that sets out how federal agencies make regulations.

That borrowed definition is broad. A document does not need to carry the label "regulation" to fall within it. What matters is whether the document meets the statutory terms.

The review law then carves out three categories. The first is rules of particular applicability. These are actions aimed at a specific party or circumstance rather than at the public generally.

The second exclusion covers rules about internal agency management. Such rules stay outside the law only if they do not substantially affect parties outside the agency. A directive that reaches no one beyond the agency's own operations would meet that condition.

The third exclusion covers rules of agency organization, procedure or practice. A similar condition attaches. These rules escape review only if they do not substantially affect the rights or obligations of outside parties.

The qualifier in the last two exclusions limits their reach. A procedural or management rule that changes what outside parties must do, or what they are entitled to, does not fit either exemption. Such a rule remains subject to review.

Guidance documents have been a point of dispute. Agencies issue guidance to explain how they read a statute or how they intend to apply it. Whether a given guidance document qualifies as a rule under the review law has been contested.

The Government Accountability Office has issued opinions on such questions. Those opinions address whether a particular agency document meets the statute's definition of a rule. Each opinion applies the statutory definition and its three exclusions to one specific document.

The definition sets the threshold for everything else in the statute. An agency action that falls outside it cannot be overturned through a joint resolution of disapproval under this law.

Federal agencies must send each new rule, along with a report on it, to both chambers of Congress and to the Comptroller General before the rule can take effect. That delivery opens Congress's chance to review the rule under the Congressional Review Act.

The Comptroller General heads the Government Accountability Office, or GAO. The submission therefore goes to three recipients: the House, the Senate and the head of the GAO. Each receives the rule itself and the agency's report on it.

The duty is not limited to the costliest regulations. It applies to every action that meets the law's definition of a rule, whether or not the rule is classified as major. A routine regulation and a sweeping one face the same delivery requirement.

Finalizing a regulation is not the agency's last step. A rule cannot take effect until the agency has completed this submission. The requirement ties a new rule's legal effect to Congress being told about it.

Congress treats receipt of the rule as the start of its review clock. The deadline for introducing a disapproval resolution counts forward from that date. The window for the Senate's expedited procedure also runs from receipt, together with the rule's publication.

The date of receipt therefore anchors the whole review. Every later procedural deadline depends on it. A lawmaker weighing a challenge first needs to know when Congress received the rule.

Rules that agencies never formally submitted have been a recurring issue under the law. The problem follows from how the review clock works. If Congress never formally receives a rule, the event that starts the review period has not happened. The delivery step is what links an agency's rulemaking to Congress's power to overturn it.

A major rule under the Congressional Review Act generally cannot take effect until 60 days after it is submitted to Congress or published in the Federal Register, whichever comes later. The waiting period applies only to rules that meet the law's definition of major.

An agency does not apply the label to itself. The Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs makes the determination. Its finding rests on what a rule is likely to do once it is in force.

The first trigger is a dollar threshold. A rule is major if it is likely to have an annual effect on the economy of $100 million or more.

A second trigger looks at prices. A rule also qualifies as major if it is likely to cause a major increase in costs or prices.

The third trigger covers wider economic harm. A rule qualifies if it is likely to have significant adverse effects on competition, employment, investment, productivity or innovation. Likely significant adverse effects on U.S. competitiveness also count.

Each trigger stands on its own. A rule with an expected annual effect below $100 million can still be major if it is likely to significantly harm employment or competition.

The 60-day clock runs from the later of two dates. One is the day the agency submits the rule to Congress. The other is the day the rule is published in the Federal Register. If an agency publishes a rule and submits it to Congress afterward, the countdown starts on the submission date.

The delay gives lawmakers time to examine a high-impact rule before it takes effect. Rules that fall short of the major tests are not held by this 60-day pause.

The Government Accountability Office works on a tighter deadline. It must send Congress a report on each major rule within 15 days. That deadline falls inside the 60-day waiting period.

A joint resolution of disapproval is the one tool the Congressional Review Act gives lawmakers to cancel a federal agency rule. The statute fixes its wording: Congress disapproves a named rule, and that rule shall have no force or effect.

The prescribed text is a single sentence with blanks. It states that Congress disapproves the rule submitted by a named agency relating to a stated subject, "and such rule shall have no force or effect." Drafters fill in the agency and the rule. They add nothing else.

That fixed text sets the resolution apart from an ordinary bill. Lawmakers do not draft new policy language or amend the regulation section by section. They name one rule and reject it.

The design narrows what a resolution can do. It cannot strike some provisions of a regulation and keep others. It cannot substitute different requirements. The only outcome it offers is disapproval of the named rule in full.

That all-or-nothing structure shapes the vote itself. A lawmaker who supports most of a regulation but objects to one provision cannot use the resolution to fix that provision. The choice is to keep the entire rule or cancel the entire rule.

The uniform wording also keeps the debate narrow. Every resolution follows the same template, so there is no policy language to negotiate. The question before each chamber is whether the named rule should stand.

Because the prescribed text names one rule, lawmakers who object to several agency regulations draft a separate resolution for each one. Each of those resolutions then moves through Congress on its own and succeeds or fails on its own.

Timing is the second constraint. A member must introduce the resolution within 60 days of continuous session after Congress receives the rule. The count runs in days of continuous session, not calendar days.

That distinction means the deadline cannot be read off a calendar. The window depends on when Congress formally received the rule. It also depends on how many days Congress remains in continuous session afterward.

The Congressional Review Act gives the Senate a fast track for disapproving agency rules. A discharge petition signed by 30 senators can pull a resolution out of committee. A simple majority can then pass it, and it cannot be filibustered.

That combination sets the Senate path apart from the route most legislation travels. Ordinary bills usually depend on committee action to reach the floor. They can also be held up by extended debate on the floor.

The discharge petition addresses the first obstacle. A committee that does not act on a joint resolution of disapproval cannot keep it from moving. Once 30 senators sign the petition, the measure comes out of committee and heads toward a floor vote.

Floor debate on the resolution is limited under the statute. That limit addresses the second obstacle. Senators who oppose the resolution cannot use open-ended debate to prevent a final vote from taking place.

No supermajority is needed at the final vote. A party holding a narrow Senate majority can pass a disapproval resolution with its own members' votes. Support from the minority party is not required.

The expedited process is available for a limited time. It lasts for 60 Senate session days after the rule is received by Congress and published.

A session day counts only when the Senate actually meets. Recesses and adjournments do not add to the total. As a result, the window can run longer than 60 calendar days.

The count depends on two events, receipt and publication. A rule that has reached Congress but has not yet been published has not started the Senate window.

Once the 60 session days pass, the fast-track procedures no longer apply to that rule. Senators who want to use the discharge petition and the majority-vote threshold must act inside the window.

The Senate fast track governs only one chamber. A disapproval resolution passed this way still needs approval from the House and the president before it becomes law.

A Congressional Review Act resolution must pass the House and the Senate and then win the president's signature to cancel a federal rule. Unlike the Senate, the House gets no statutory fast track for these measures.

The difference between the chambers is written into the law itself. The statute builds an expedited path for the Senate. It provides no matching procedure for the House. A disapproval resolution introduced in the House gets no special protection from the Congressional Review Act.

House leadership fills that gap with its own tools. The usual route is a special rule reported by the Rules Committee. That special rule sets the terms for bringing the disapproval resolution to the floor. As a result, the timing of a House vote rests with the chamber's leadership rather than with a deadline in the statute.

Passage in one chamber is not enough. Both the House and the Senate must approve the same resolution before it reaches the president.

The president then has two choices: sign the resolution or veto it. A signature enacts the disapproval. A veto returns the measure to Congress.

Overriding a veto takes a two-thirds vote in each chamber. Both the House and the Senate must clear that threshold separately. A resolution that passed either chamber by a narrow margin will not reach two-thirds without added support. That math makes a veto difficult to overcome.

The presidential step shapes how the law works in practice. A president is unlikely to approve a resolution that cancels a rule from the president's own administration. Successful use of the Congressional Review Act has mostly come under one set of conditions.

The president and the congressional majorities belong to the same party. The targeted rule came from a predecessor's administration. Under that alignment, both chambers can pass a disapproval resolution and the president can sign it. That pattern links the law's real-world use to changes in party control of the White House.

Once a Congressional Review Act disapproval becomes law, the targeted rule is treated as though it never took effect. The agency that wrote it also loses the power to reissue a rule that is "substantially the same" unless a later law specifically authorizes it.

Those two consequences point in opposite directions in time. One erases the rule's past. The other limits what the agency can do next on the same subject.

The "never took effect" treatment reaches backward. The rule does not simply end on the date the resolution becomes law. For legal purposes, it is handled as if it had never been in force.

The reissue bar reaches forward. An agency cannot return months later with a near-copy of the disapproved rule. To write something substantially similar, it needs Congress to pass a new law that specifically allows it. Like any other legislation, that law must clear both chambers. It must then either win the president's signature or survive a veto.

The open question is the phrase itself. The statute does not define "substantially the same." It offers no test for how far a replacement must depart from the original before it falls outside the ban.

That gap leaves the scope of the restriction unsettled. An agency weighing a new rule on a subject Congress has already disapproved has no statutory definition to consult. It has to judge for itself how close is too close.

The Congressional Review Act adds a further limit. It states that actions and omissions under the law are not subject to judicial review. That language narrows the usual path of asking a court to decide what a statute requires.

The result pairs a firm prohibition with an undefined standard. A disapproved rule cannot come back on the agency's own authority. The boundary between a barred near-copy and a permissible new rule remains unsettled in the statute's text.

The Congressional Review Act's lookback period gives a new session of Congress a chance to review rules that arrived late in the previous one. It applies to rules submitted within 60 legislative days in the House, or 60 session days in the Senate, before sine die adjournment.

Sine die adjournment is the formal end of a session. A chamber adjourns without setting a date to return. The lookback window counts backward from that point.

Rules caught in the window are treated as though they were submitted on the 15th session day of the next session. That legal fiction resets the timing. The review clock starts over in the new session.

The restart reaches the introduction deadline too. Lawmakers have 60 days of continuous session after a rule is received to introduce a joint resolution of disapproval. Under the lookback, that period runs from the 15th session day of the new session.

The Senate's fast-track window restarts as well. Senators get a fresh 60 session days to use the expedited procedure against rules that reached Congress in the final stretch of the prior session.

The window is measured in days a chamber actually meets, not calendar days. The House count uses legislative days. The Senate count uses session days. Each chamber keeps its own calendar, so the two windows can open on different dates.

No one can pin down the start of the lookback window while a session is still under way. It depends on how many days each chamber meets before final adjournment. The start date is fixed only after the fact, once the calendar is complete.

That uncertainty matters for agencies finishing rules late in a session. A rule sent to Congress near the end of a session may or may not fall inside the window. The answer turns on a schedule that has not yet played out.

Rules that arrive earlier in a session do not get this second chance. They follow the ordinary review timeline instead.

Congress first used the Congressional Review Act successfully in 2001, overturning the Occupational Safety and Health Administration's ergonomics rule. Successful use then stayed rare until 2017, when a Republican Congress and President Donald Trump overturned many Obama-era rules.

The ergonomics rule came from OSHA, the workplace safety agency within the Department of Labor. Congress passed a joint resolution of disapproval against it. President George W. Bush signed the measure on March 20, 2001. With his signature, the resolution became law and the rule was canceled.

That first success arrived just under five years after President Bill Clinton signed the CRA on March 29, 1996. For that entire stretch, the statute had not canceled a single regulation.

The disapproval had lasting consequences for OSHA. Under the law, the ergonomics rule is treated as though it never took effect. The agency also cannot issue a rule that is "substantially the same" unless a later act of Congress specifically authorizes it.

For years after 2001, the ergonomics repeal stood as the only successful use of the CRA. Lawmakers kept the power to introduce disapproval resolutions throughout that period.

Use of the statute rose sharply in 2017, 16 years after the ergonomics vote. President Donald Trump took office that year alongside a Republican Congress. Those majorities used the CRA to overturn many rules finalized under the Obama administration.

The 2017 surge fit the conditions under which the CRA has mostly worked. The president belonged to the same party as the congressional majorities and was willing to sign their disapprovals. The rules in question had been issued by a predecessor rather than by the sitting administration.

That alignment matters because the president can veto a disapproval resolution like any other joint resolution. Overriding a veto requires two-thirds of each chamber. Without shared party control, a resolution that clears both chambers can still fail at the president's desk.
