Scientists tracked millions of phones to uncover patterns in carbon emissions

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Scientists used anonymized cell phone mobility data to estimate carbon emissions, testing the approach across four countries.

When COVID-19 lockdowns froze the planet in early 2020, carbon emissions plummeted almost overnight.

The problem? The instruments tracking those emissions couldn’t keep up.

Conventional inventories lag reality by months. Satellite inversions come with their own trade-offs between resolution and coverage.

A new study explores another approach: using people’s movements to help estimate carbon emissions.

The research was led by Mengning Wang and Xin Lu from China’s National University of Defense Technology.

Collaborators included Xiaoling Zhang from the University of Hong Kong and scholars from various international institutions.

The researchers turned anonymized cell phone signals from 318 million Chinese mobile users into what amounts to a near-real-time carbon meter.

They tested the framework across four countries.

Commuting, deliveries, business trips, and holiday travel often generate carbon emissions.

The researchers reasoned that if so many carbon-producing processes involve physically moving people and goods, then the movement itself might serve as a fast measuring stick.

They took anonymized signaling data from China Unicom and converted it into daily intercity mobility networks.

Each city became a node and each flow of people between cities became an edge.

From these networks they extracted structural features – how central a city is, how tightly clustered its connections are, how many links it maintains – and combined those with population and geographic data.

Then they trained machine learning models against the GRACED dataset, which tracks daily CO2 emissions across seven sectors: power, industry, residential consumption, ground transport, domestic aviation, international aviation, and shipping.

The window researchers studied was January and February 2020. It’s hard to imagine a harder test.

Spring Festival travel sent 68.74 million daily intercity trips surging through the network. Then lockdowns hit.

Mobility crashed by 71.3%. Multi-source carbon emissions dropped from an average of roughly 356.5 million kilograms of carbon per day to a minimum of 209.6 million – a 41.2% collapse. The whole thing happened in weeks.

And yet the correlation between daily national mobility and CO2 emissions held firm at r = 0.89. At the city level, it was a more modest but still significant r = 0.49.

The best-performing model – LightGBM, a tree-based ensemble – achieved R² values of 1.00 for multi-source emissions, 0.99 for domestic aviation, and 0.98 for ground transportation across 366 cities and 60 days.

Those are extraordinary numbers for something as messy as urban carbon output.

The real surprise came when the framework crossed borders.

Using open-source mobility datasets, the team applied the same approach to Italy, the continental United States, and Mexico.

The model reached R² values of 0.91, 0.95, and 0.99 respectively against the GRACED inventory.

Tested against a completely independent dataset – ODIAC, which derives emissions from nighttime light imagery and power-plant data at 1 km resolution – it still scored between 0.90 and 0.99.

Four countries, two independent benchmarks, consistent accuracy.

Something unexpected emerged from the mobility data. The Louvain community detection algorithm carved China’s 366 cities into 11 functional clusters.

These are groupings defined not by provincial borders but by how people actually move between them. Beijing-Tianjin-Hebei formed one cluster, pumping out 62.75 million kilograms of carbon daily.

The Yangtze River Delta formed another, at 49.71 million.

These clusters aren’t just descriptive. More central, more connected clusters emit more carbon.

But here’s the policy hook. China’s existing climate governance tends to target a handful of major emitting cities while overlooking smaller ones that sit in structurally critical positions within the network.

The mobility-defined clusters offer something different: a template for coordinated regional decarbonization that matches how economies actually function rather than how administrative maps happen to be drawn.

The authors aren’t overselling this. They call mobility a “partial but observable proxy” – a complement to inventories, not a replacement.

The Chinese analysis covers a single extraordinary two-month window. The data captures intercity movement, not what happens within a city.

Mobility alone may not fully capture emissions changes driven by seasonal energy demand, holiday schedules, or weekly cycles.

There are subtler problems too. Cellular signaling data inherits all the biases of telecom coverage – regional disparities, operator market share, sampling artifacts.

And the fundamental link between mobility and emissions could shift as remote work spreads, transport electrifies, and travel patterns reshape.

A correlation forged during the most dramatic behavioral disruption in modern history might not hold the same way during a quiet Tuesday in 2030.

Still, the core appeal is hard to argue with. Standard emissions inventories are accurate but glacially slow.

With timely access to mobility data, the framework could provide frequently updated emissions estimates.

If it scales, climate policymakers would gain a behavior-informed reading of the present rather than a months-old accounting of the past.

The team calls for fusing their mobility approach with remote sensing, energy structure data, and meteorological variables.

The ambition is to make carbon monitoring something closer to weather forecasting: imperfect, always updating, but fast enough to actually be useful.

The study is published in the journal The Innovation.

To Penny, or Not to Penny?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Few shoes are as essential in the modern menswear landscape as the classic leather loafer. Whether you’re dressing up or dressing down, it is the “the Swiss Army Knife of shoes,” an any-occasion solution to many footwear conundrums. But within the broader loafer genus, one particular species—the penny loafer—includes a divisive design feature: a small, coin-shaped slot in the leather strap “saddle” across the vamp of the shoe.

As the lore goes, Ivy Style pioneers and sock-hopping teens in the mid-20th century liked to store a penny in their shoes as “payphone change.” But now that payphones are a thing of the misty-eyed past and the American one-cent penny is no longer minted for circulation, is it still cool to put a penny (or any other coin, or coin-shaped object) in your penny loafers? What is the line between timeless and passé?

We invited two of GQ’s most well-heeled staffers, associate grooming and wellness editor Tyler Chin and senior style commerce writer Gerald Ortiz, to ponder the pros and cons of the penny loafer.

Mahershala Ali, an ostensible penny proponent, in London on October 19.

Tyler Chin, associate grooming and wellness editor: Sure! I’ve seen so many guys wearing loafers these days, and yet still haven’t seen any of them harking back to the shoe’s origins with a penny in them. Maybe I’m not looking hard enough or I’m not in circles where they are doing that, but it’s an inoffensive and easy way to accessorize the shoe. Plus, I had an English teacher in high school who did it, and while she wasn’t particularly cool, I did think the sartorial choice was neat.

Gerald Ortiz, senior style commerce writer: No. I feel like guys who put pennies in their loafers are either try-hards, or just learned about that particular piece of prep trivia. The only acceptable instance to wear a penny in your loafers is if you’re really about that prep/Ivy style, like Preppy Pete, or the guys who frequent Alfargo’s Market (shout out).

TC: I think the penny would be the most boring and obvious choice. If a quarter could fit in there, I think it’d be so funny if you were at a vending machine and short a few cents and settled up with the coin in your shoe. One time, the button on my pants fell off on the street and I lost it, but imagine I had a spare button in my shoe? I could reattach it right there and then! In the hypothetical I’d also need to be toting around a sewing kit, but hey, I’d love to think I could be MacGyver in that scenario.

GO: It doesn’t have to be anything at all. But if you’re going to do it, I don’t see why it couldn’t be another coin or object. It could be a Chuck E. Cheese token. Whatever you put in the slot, it would still be wack.

TC: I just got back from a trip to Tokyo, and I had a lot of leftover yen coins. Two of them—the five and 50 yen—have a hole on them, which differentiates them from your boring American coin. I’d throw one of them in my shoe and be one of those people who never stops talking about their travel abroad.

GO: I saw on Instagram someone stick a hundred dollar bill into their loafer and that was funny for a second. Then I scrolled on to the next meme.

TC: I’m pretty sure a coin that is that close to the ground will surely have a patina before the end of the day. Also, I can’t remember the last time I’ve seen a shiny new coin—who carries loose change these days anyway?!

GO: You know, it might be kind of cool to see a penny completely covered in verdigris slotted into your loafers. But maybe the coin should match the sheen of your loafers. I dunno, just don’t do it and you won’t have to think about it.

TC: Everyone I know is very familiar with my love of penny loafers. Lately I’ve been wearing them with bootcut jeans that are just a little longer than usual, so they have a nice drape over the shoe. Top-wise, I’d go with a tucked-in button up, T-shirt, or sweater. Pretty much anything that would go well with sneakers would be equally suited for wearing with loafers. Which is nearly all outfits.

GO: Penniless or not, a pair of wide khakis with a clean-to-slight break and a striped button-up is a failsafe outfit. It wouldn’t be very inventive, but it would pass muster for most conceivable situations. Vintage straight-leg jeans with good fades, a fucked-up band tee, and a sportcoat is also a great look. But the best looks are always the unexpected pairings.

TC: It’s sort of like how guys will go on and on about having functional sleeve buttons on a suit jacket. Is there any situation where you’ll actually need to unbutton those or do you just leave one or two undone to “stunt” on your unimpressed bros? If you really want to put a coin in your shoe for the sake of accessorizing your footwear, then by all means get a shoe with a functioning penny slot and use it! But if you’re doing it just so you can say “Yeah, these joints are the real deal!” and use it as an excuse to talk about the history of the shoe and why yours are superior, then I’d rather you wear lace-ups.

GO: I think it’s fine, even preferable to have a faux penny slot. It’s like how jeans still come with the watch pocket. It’s purely vestigial at this point, but would feel incomplete without it.

TC: One of these days, I’ll be telling my great-great grandchildren about the origins of the penny loafer and they’ll be confused because they would’ve never heard or seen the one-cent coin before. I’ll whip one out of my shoe and instead of being impressed they’ll chalk it up to another one of Old Man Ty’s tales from his youth, and tuck me back into the nursing home they checked me into.

GO: Lol. I guess it’ll make the penny more covetable. But it’ll be a long time before the penny becomes that desirable. That said, I love this British brand Six 95 that’s designing cool pieces using coins, so I can absolutely see more labels farming pennies for their clothes and accessories.

TC: Men will break up anything other than a toxic relationship. Loafers did nothing wrong and sometimes it’s just the person wearing them that needs to hit the curb.

GO: It makes sense. It’s like any trend, really. Even if they’re as versatile as we’ve been propping them up to be, loafers reached an inflection point where, for some guys, they’re just not the vibe anymore. But I don’t think they’ll dip so low to be thought of as cringe or as an emblem of the 2020s. They’re as classic as it gets and they’ll go through their own ebbs and flows.

TC: A quarter to increase my net worth 25-fold.

Where to Eat During AFM 2026: Century City and Beverly Hills’ Best Restaurants

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

AFM 2026 is in the center of the independent film business dealmaking world and close to many of the entertainment biz’s favorite restaurants. Century City and nearby Beverly Hills are home to the major agencies, law firms and financing entities, which equals power lunching and see-and-be-seen gathering spots close to the confab. Throw in some legendary eateries, and AFMers are perfectly positioned to dine where Hollywood dealmakers, agency foot soldiers and bold names eat out.Century City’s dining options start at the host venue: Birdie G’s is the Fairmont Century Plaza’s new full-service restaurant. Expect classic American cooking in a relaxed setting. There will be mobile coffee stations and daily grab-and-go items near the Fairmont’s lower-level conference rooms. Choices include hearty breakfast burritos, creative salads and an array of sandwiches.At the adjacent Westfield Century City, where AFM badge holders can take advantage of a discount program, more than 65 food outlets populate the multi-level, open-air mall.Specializing in dim sum, Din Tai Fung (10250 Santa Monica Blvd., second level, dtf.com) excels in Taiwan-style, handmade soup dumplings and flavorful noodle dishes. Waits can be extensive, so sit at the bar, reserve ahead or use yelp.com to join the real-time waitlist. Related Stories Pixar Names Jonathan Garson as President; Jim Morris Moves to Emeritus Role

Terra, the restaurant at Eataly Los Angeles (10250 Santa Monica Blvd., Eataly.com), boasts ample outdoor seating to enjoy Terra’s wood-fired Italian dishes and craft cocktails. The Monday-Friday happy hour on the bar terrace features $10 glasses of wine, cocktails and Aperol spritzes paired with grilled appetizers.

On the lower level of the nearby Century Building, Hinoki & the Bird (10 W. Century Dr., hinokiandthebird.com) attracts the high-powered lunch crowd, including CAA agents along with their clients and execs from the Fox studio lot. Serving globally inspired California cuisine, lunch favorites range from sashimi starters to the ever-popular crispy jidori chicken to the kale salad topped with spiced cashews. Enticing dinner entrees include the hinoki-scented black cod and dry-aged Peking duck. The tucked-away location provides privacy; the sheltered, heated outdoor patio is open year-round. Reservations are highly recommended. Monday-Friday happy hour runs 5-7 p.m.

Beverly Hills excels at chic hotel dining. Opened in May, Gemma on the rooftop of the Waldorf Astoria Beverly Hills (9850 Wilshire Blvd., waldorfastoriabeverlyhills.com) has unequaled views across Beverly Hills. The upscale pan-Asian fare (highlights: dim sum, seafood raw bar, lobster burger and Singaporean noodles) and fanciful cocktails more than satisfy the rooftop’s stylish crowd. Edoardo Baldi’s Tuscan steakhouse Baldi (baldi.com) brings the restaurateur’s signature hospitality and famed pasta to the Waldorf’s first floor.

The Maybourne Beverly Hills (225 N. Canon Dr., maybournebeverlyhills.com) boasts a caviar and oyster bar at its cozy Maybourne Bar. Tradition and old-school service reign on the leafy patio of the Beverly Hills Hotel’s Polo Lounge (9641 Sunset Blvd., dorchestercollection.com), a go-to for Hollywood’s elite for decades.

Mastro’s is celebrating 25 years, and the Garden at the Penthouse at Mastro’s Steakhouse Beverly Hills (246 Canon Dr., mastrosrestaurants.com) welcomes power players like Michael B. Jordan, Emily Blunt and John Krasinski to its clubby third-floor indoor-outdoor dining room. For dinner, there are massive steaks and chops and a heady assortment of seafood.

For those seeking a flowery, photo-ready indoor-outdoor space that feels miles away, take the world’s prettiest elevator to the rooftop restaurant Monsieur Dior by Dominique Crenn (323 N. Rodeo Dr., monsieurdiorbeverlyhills.com). The inventive lunch, dinner and lounge menus come from Michelin-starred restaurateur/chef Dominique Crenn, paired with elegant interiors by architect Peter Marino. Sip on a glass of Chateau Minuty, the house rosé wine from Provence, France, to embrace the French ambiance.

The businessperson’s lunch crowd flocks to Avra Beverly Hills (233 N. Beverly Dr., theavragroup.com) for the prix fixe, three-course Mediterranean-style lunch special priced at $41.95. Start with a Greek salad, then move on to the grilled salmon and then finish with a baklava, all served in Avra’s bright, open-air setting populated by the city’s elite.
